such is the vajra of the supreme vehicle:
a treasure from which inexhaustible maṇḍalas arise,
a precious wish-fulfilling jewel,
utterly clear without being polished.
Though it has remained for countless eons,
its body has the form of a youth in full bloom.
Free of birth and death, it is the vajra
treasure from which all things arise,
endowed with perfect qualities.
Having accomplished unsurpassed awakening,
may vajra wisdom be spontaneously present!
May the two great accumulations be completed!
As the Teacher of all,
having attained the state of omniscience,
may one hold mastery over the buddhafields!
May all who realize this, the vajra family,
also accomplish body, speech, and mind!

Yogis endowed with skillful means and wisdom should at all times enjoy the five sense pleasures and engage in dance.

This concludes the seventy-first chapter, on dance, song, and play, from The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality.

Then the Tathāgata brought forth the maṇḍala of body from the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, and spoke these words.

From the expanse endowed with suchness,
illusion and net become joined together,
and through the appearance of the vajra light of the supreme body,
the concordant vajra appears of itself.
The bodies of the six streams of saṃsāra that appear
are karmic appearances concordant with conceptual thought.
Likewise, the stainless supreme body
is summarized as the five bodies of the Sugata:
the Blessed One, the form of awakening,
the unsurpassed form, and the vajra body,
these and other inconceivable bodies.
Showing bodies in the ten directions and the four times,
showing bodies to tame beings through skillful means,
the vajra body of supreme awakening
shows bodies in realms free of attachment,
and appears without stirring from the expanse.
The appearing body is the expanse of suchness.
It does not show only pure bodies:
to all those whose thoughts go astray
it also shows bodies that tame each one individually.
Then it is the body without birth or death,
the lotus body unattached to karma.
Though liberated and unliberated are various,
it appears in variety yet does not abide;
there is neither appearance nor nonappearance.
The supreme family that realizes this
is designated vajra.
However many inconceivable bodies are shown,
all without exception are gathered into the vajra.
What is also called the wheel,
these and other inconceivable bodies,
arose from the center of the skillful means of awakening.
If one knows all the various bodies
to be like rainbows and the moon in water,
then from the expanse itself, it is nothing but the appearance of the expanse.
Mind itself appears as the maṇḍala of speech;
speech itself is the maṇḍala of body;
indivisible, so they are, entirely.
Those who wish to accomplish the excellent maṇḍala
should always, in all three times,
since self and what is one's own are indivisible,
regard this as the maṇḍala from the very beginning.
That this is the supreme accomplishment,
the All-Victorious One has thus declared.

"Bringing Forth the Maṇḍala of Body," from the Great King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion. It is said that the chapter on the profound maṇḍala of body is explained in the Later King of Tantras and was not translated into Tibetan. This is the seventy-second chapter, on gathering into the maṇḍala of the body of the intrinsic nature.

Then the Tathāgata, from the Great King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, from the net of methods for applying oneself to the stages of samādhi and swiftly gaining the accomplishments, spoke these words:

The accomplishment of inconceivable maṇḍalas
draws forth its meaning from the nonreferential expanse.
Resting in the natural state, uncontrived, just as it is,
the spontaneously present maṇḍala arises.
If, from the nonreferential maṇḍala of the mind,
one gains mastery through samādhi,
the various emanations of wisdom are accomplished.
Every domain of activity is a maṇḍala;
the wisdom maṇḍala liberates and protects.
Enjoyment bodies and buddhafields
are accomplished through aspiration, just as one wishes.
Vast, spacious, and spontaneously present,
outer and inner, the world and its beings, are pure from the very beginning.
The realization of purity is itself the expanse;
from that comes vajra equality.
If you wish to accomplish the attainments swiftly,
meditate in a way that is very subtle and profound.
Self, what is one's own, and others,
meditate upon as the utterly nonreferential expanse.
Having grown familiar with that samādhi,
one should understand all things as illusions, as mirages,
and as the mode of emanations.
Having grown familiar with this again and again in the same way,
then meditate on the subtle samādhi.
Gather the outer and inner, the world and its beings, into hūṃ.
Whatever is to be accomplished has its own particular syllable,
yet in general, whatever one meditates on is alike in every case:
all gathers into Vajrasattva himself.
The five limbs and consciousness itself
are gathered completely into the syllable hūṃ.
So that it is as though visible to the eye of nṛ,
growing familiar with it again and again,
one should emanate many rays of light.
Having filled the ten directions and the four times,
one should dissolve them back into the hūṃ.
The hūṃ itself dissolves into the nonreferential expanse.
From the expanse into which it dissolved, a white a blazes forth.
Having meditated on it as clear and utterly brilliant,
meditate that everything becomes a.
Having grown familiar with it as suchness,
from the a emanate a sun maṇḍala.
How wonderful! Once it has clearly come about,
meditate on sun and moon as full sun and moon discs.
Meditate on a clear hrī as a lotus free of attachment.
Meditate on a celestial palace of blazing jewels.
The sun and moon seats and the celestial palace,
gather them all and meditate on them as subtle.
Likewise, through the abiding syllable hūṃ,
dwelling there as a brilliant blue,
meditate on rays of light radiating out.
Having gathered the radiating light back into the expanse,
meditate on vajra light rays radiating powerfully.
When the radiating vajra light has blazed forth clearly,
one should dissolve it into the expanse once more.
That itself becomes the body of the sattva:
meditate on it as very subtle and condensed.
Likewise, when nonduality has become clear,
meditate on a single body with vajra and bell.
Once it has become suchness itself,
meditate on it as having three heads and six arms.
Meditate also on various wrathful tamers.
According to the greater or lesser wisdom of one's intellect,
one should meditate in gradual stages,
or else one should meditate all at once,
as sun, moon, planets, and stars at a single moment
appear clearly in a limpid pool of water.
One of the supreme family who has thus grown familiar and realized
accomplishes just as one gains mastery:
flying in the sky, dwelling as a vidyādhara,
turning back the flow of rivers and stilling the wind,
passing unobstructed through mountains, cliffs, and the like,
not sinking in water, not burned by fire,
not endangered by poison or weapons,
becoming invisible and emanating many bodies,
and anything else, just as one wishes:
holding mastery over gods and demons,
lengthening and shortening lifespans and times,
producing a trichiliocosm in a mustard seed,
all is accomplished just as it is envisioned.
Snakes and vicious beasts of prey
will follow behind like dogs.
Māras, vicious beings, and the like
cannot prevail and become like servants.
Without abandoning the body, one reaches the level of a vidyādhara;
through the enjoyment of wisdom and merit
one gains the supreme assembly of the wheel of syllables.
Through the blessing of compassionate emanations
one also teaches the path of individual liberation
and also works for the benefit of all beings without exception.
Having carried out every activity,
one attains the immutable state beyond birth and death,
adorned with all the major and minor marks,
possessing the great accumulations of merit and wisdom,
and accomplishing the ultimate, where nothing remains to be done:
the dharmadhātu, with nothing to accomplish and nothing to abandon,
suchness, with no going and no coming.
If one practices and meditates in this way,
even if the result has not yet become manifest,
in all one's lifetimes and births
one will never be born into evil deeds,
one will never come to the Lesser Vehicle,
and one will become a holder of the supreme vajra family.
So it is in the words of the vajra secret.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, this is the seventy-third chapter, on obtaining the accomplishments through the lineage that is to be kept utterly secret.

Then the Tathāgata spoke these activities of the fire offerings (homa) from the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion.

First, meditate on suchness until it is clear and familiar. Then meditate on a single clear and brilliant a at your heart. When that a has become very clear, meditate that it radiates as light into the ten directions. Gather that light back into the a and dissolve it there. From ma and a, meditate on the maṇḍalas of the sun and moon, merged, brilliant, and clear. From hrī, meditate clearly on a lotus maṇḍala. Meditate on hūṃ blazing with intense light. In the maṇḍala of the heart, meditate on a single Vajrasattva a finger's length in size, together with his mudrā. Recite clearly the forty-two mantras, their syllables, and their mudrās as well. Recite also the three syllables and the five syllables. Seal the boundaries with the four syllables of abiding. Perform fire offerings constantly, and perform the gathering feasts and offerings as well. For the sake of those unable to do many, it is acceptable to perform whichever fire offering one can.

As the principal deity at the center of the maṇḍala: for the general rite, Vajrasattva; for the wrathful rite, Vajra as well; for magnetizing, Padma Heruka; for enriching, Ratna Heruka; for pacifying, Buddha Heruka. For the invitation, one invites from the principal deity of whichever fire offering one is performing, saying ehyehi mahākāruṇīka. When presenting offerings and the argha: for the wrathful rite, at the head of the mantra string, hūṃ; for the peaceful rite, at the head of the string, aṃ; apply the same to those in between. For the invitation, from whichever principal deity one is practicing, for pacifying add śāntiṃ; for the general rite, complete each deity's own mantra. The wrathful rite faces south, magnetizing west, enriching north, and pacifying east; for the general rite, any direction will do. The hearths are a triangle and an open-mouthed skull cup, a crescent, a lotus, and the one free of attachment. Of the seven kinds of mudrā there are the great mudrā, the mudrā of the nature of reality, the samaya mudrā, the action mudrā, the mudrā of skillful means and wisdom, the mudrā of samādhi, and the mudrā of superior samādhi.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, this is the seventy-fourth chapter, on the four kinds of fire offering.

Then the Tathāgata, from the Great King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, also explained the fifteen wondrous samayas in brief, as follows:

Through the wisdom of omniscience,
in the very state where all is equal,
these are gathered into the expanse as a single taste;
never has it been said that they fail to become the aim.
The desire for supreme qualities:
since all that appears and exists, without exception,
is joined with the realm of equality,
one engages in it in the manner of nonattachment.
This is the pure and excellent path,
the ground for going to supreme awakening;
it becomes an inexhaustible treasure of great bliss,
the vajra body that nothing can assail.
But desire, which is affliction and suffering,
becomes obscured by body and food,
and one goes from place to place in the realms of the hungry ghosts,
tormented by thirst and hunger,
forever seeing pus, blood, and foul vomit,
without even the power to enjoy happiness,
and guarded by the harsh and vicious.
The anger of great compassion
cuts off the door to the sorrow of saṃsāra
and leads beings into the path of the Sugata,
so that they come to possess the genuine meaning.
Displaying wrath in the manner of compassion,
it places them in the state of perfect equality.
Through the peace of Vajrasattva
genuine, supreme bliss is accomplished.
The bliss of clear wisdom:
self and what is one's own are indivisible;
it plays in the expanse without intrinsic nature,
the state in which nothing at all can be expressed,
possessing a body that pervades everything,
the utterly luminous, pure path.
It purifies the obscurations of bodhisattvas
and clears away the poison of the afflictions of sentient beings.
The pride that purifies all realms
is the treasury that gives birth to good qualities.
Where there is no I, no self, and nothing to apprehend,
who would give rise to pride toward that?
One accomplishes the utterly victorious leader of the herd,
whom hosts of fourfold armies cannot conquer,
and attains the samādhi of heroic going
that crushes the gods, demons, and armies of Māra.
Jealousy, applied to the meaning of narrow and vast:
if one realizes the meaning of no I and no self,
narrow and vast are not two,
and the vast maṇḍala of supreme bliss is accomplished.
But if one does not understand the reality of jealousy
and grasps at an I and a self,
one is born in the abode of the asuras,
and from there goes to the realm of the hungry ghosts.
Even if one finds land and a place,
it becomes very unhappy and cramped.
Moreover, over all one's enjoyments
one has no control and falls under the power of others.
In short, affliction and suffering are produced.
The genuine realization of jealousy
is to be jealous of grasping at an I and a self
and of all that is inverted,
and to join all things in nonduality.
Then supreme bliss, too, will be accomplished,
the meaning of nonduality will be realized,
one dissolves into the expanse without I or self,
and the spontaneously present supreme aim will be accomplished.
If one constantly meditates on the deity in one's mind,
through cause and result spontaneously present,
one gains mastery over all things
and will be born in the abode of the vidyādharas.
What the vajra family is to practice
is brought to completion through liberation and union.
Union itself is liberation;
by their very nature they are union and liberation;
in liberation itself there is union.
There is union and liberation through samādhi,
union and liberation at the time of sense objects,
union and liberation by those of great skillful means,
and union and liberation made manifest.
Or else, union and liberation of the self and so forth:
one joins the expanse of wisdom with the self.
The emanations of great skillful means are inconceivable;
one liberates all things into the expanse, into the expanse.
The activities of play are inconceivable;
the emanations of compassion are inconceivable;
the activities of union and liberation are perfected in oneself.
Emanations of the mind of awakening radiate into the ten directions;
they empty the abodes of the lower realms,
bring about the attainment of the bliss of the vidyādharas,
and purify the unsurpassed realms.
Body and speech, spontaneously present, tame the ten directions.
Proclaiming the name "Vajra,"
they establish even those of the Lesser Vehicle in the vajra,
and the inexhaustible treasure is accomplished as well.
The body of the vajra knowledge-holder,
the body of the equality in which all is indivisible,
the supreme wisdom of omniscience,
through immeasurable emanations of compassion,
knows the six classes of beings in all times and places without exception.
Within beings themselves is the very body;
it knows the afflictions as the path of awakening;
saṃsāra itself is the abode of bliss.
The dharmadhātu and the full extent of sentient beings
are indivisible in genuine reality itself.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, this is the seventy-fifth chapter, which also explains the fifteen wondrous samayas in this way.

Then the Tathāgata, from the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, uttered this inspired utterance, himself to himself, on this very elaboration of the samayas.

The samaya called "Vajra of Elaboration":
Within the compassion of the vajra of anger,
which cuts off the abodes of the hells,
the eight and eight, sixteen, are joined into a pure realm.
For those in the hot hells, the medicine of coolness;
for those in the cold hells, the medicine of warmth;
balancing cool and warm, soft and rough:
thus it displays the blessing of compassion.
The blessing is wondrous and utterly marvelous.

The samaya called "Wisdom of Attachment":
In the manner of nonattachment,
one should enjoy all desirable things.
Just as one tastes the flavor of bliss,
in the very experience of feeling there is no intrinsic nature.
Through the wisdom free of attachment
the abodes of the hungry ghosts are cut off,
and it shows that both afflictions,
of being obscured by body and by food, are absent.

The samaya called "Delusion":
Through the luminosity of great wisdom,
nondual, without I and without self,
the suffering of those without memory or speech is cleared away.
Having purified the obscurations into nondelusion,
through the compassion of clear wisdom
it frees from the afflictions without agent or action,
and it empties the abodes of the animals.
Thus it lights the lamp of compassion.

The samaya called "Jealousy":
Through the wisdom of great vastness,
for those who grasp at an I and a self,
compassion joins them evenly in nonduality,
and narrow and vast are both absent.
For one who possesses a vast and spacious mind,
there is nothing that is not included in this.
It joins all things in vastness itself,
and it empties the realms of the asuras.

The samaya called "Pride, Supreme Bliss":
In the primordial equality of the nature of reality,
there is no such thing as "I" and "you."
All things are of a single taste in the expanse;
to its nondual character nothing can be added.
Since it is the state without great or small,
it is shown that there is nothing to be done.
Through the realization of the selflessness of pride,
the abodes of the haughty are emptied.

The samaya called "Natural Luminosity":
Whatever exists in this world,
all that holds to names and conventions,
if one deconstructs their individual characteristics,
is without intrinsic nature: it is reality itself.
Through the wisdom of the great self-arisen,
all things are joined into buddhafields,
uncontrived, unmade, naturally clear:
the buddhafield, luminous from the very beginning.

The samaya called "The Very Maṇḍala":
Abiding in the intrinsic nature itself,
made by no one, self-arisen,
it joins all things into suchness
and dissolves them into the state of the nondual expanse.
Through the maṇḍala of self-arisen wisdom
all without exception are gathered into suchness.
Gathering and not gathering are both absent:
it is reality itself.

The samaya called "The State of Bliss":
All things without exception are utterly pure;
because they are not other than their own expanse,
bliss and nonbliss are both absent.
All things are of a single taste in the expanse.
Since the state of suchness prevails,
reality itself has spoken thus.
Explaining and listening are both absent as well;
both are gathered into suchness.

The samaya called "Supreme Victor of the Four Times":
The supreme, unsurpassed wisdom
holds mastery over all things
and joins all things in equality.
All that is past and not yet past,
what will arise and what is uncertain to arise,
through the distinction of wisdom that realizes them,
are joined together at once in equality,
where the four times have no before and after.

The samaya called "Spontaneous Presence":
The transcendent and the worldly
are all indivisible awakening.
Phenomena pure from the very beginning,
uncontrived and spontaneously present:
this is the great path of awakening.
All things are gathered into indivisibility itself.
Accomplishing the aim of awakening is complete from the beginning;
all things dissolve into the realm of equality.

The samaya called "Vajra Birthplace":
Certain to be of the family of the supreme Great Vehicle,
being of that family, one is held by the teaching of awakening.
If one knows the expanse in which all is equal,
it is the birthplace of all the supreme families.
In birth itself there is no birth;
there is no karma and no karmic imprint.
Unstained by anything, it conquers all;
it is also the ground without birth or death.

The samaya called "Arraying the Fields of Awakening":
In the ten directions, endless and boundless,
the inconceivable appearances and sounds,
the outer and inner abodes,
dissolve into the expanse of suchness.
In short, in all the realms without exception,
elaborated by the ornaments of wisdom
that array precious qualities,
the qualities of those fields are inconceivable.

The samaya called "Abode of Wisdom":
Wisdom free of apprehended and apprehender
is the abode of all the tathāgatas.
Those who wish to accomplish the great secret mantra
attain the wisdom free of apprehended and apprehender.
The dharmadhātu and sentient beings
are one in suchness, without duality.
The person who realizes this
is said to be the abode of the vajra.

The samaya called "Supreme Fearless Victor":
Purification and affliction
do not exist as two separate things;
affliction cannot be corrected by an antidote.
Since all things are of a single taste in the expanse,
in it there is no fear and no antidote.
Unassailable by anything and unsurpassed,
it is also the field of supreme bliss,
without I and without self, the supreme among victors.

The samaya called "Stable Celestial Palace":
Joining all of the ten directions and the four times
into the field of the Victor,
its narrowness and breadth cannot be measured.
Its mark is like the crown protuberance of a buddha;
it is accomplished from the cause of wisdom;
it is accomplished just as it is envisioned.
Accomplished from the exhaustion of defilements,
it is the dharmadhātu, never subject to fear.

The samaya called "Joining All Variety":
Since it is not the way of outcasts,
it is accomplished just as one intends.
Since awareness has become self-governing,
one may make things empty or not empty.
Since all is the state of indivisibility,
colors, shapes, and precious jewels
pervade everything, in the manner of a mustard seed:
inconceivable, without edge or center.

The samaya called "The Power of the Guide's Abode":
Through inconceivable ignorance and wrong conceptions,
the inconceivable world systems,
both the cause and the result of saṃsāra,
are instants without basis or root.
If one knows the genuine mind of awakening,
ignorance, its cause and its result, are utterly purified.
The afflictions themselves are awakening,
and all things without exception are the abode of bliss.

The samaya called "Showing the Five Paths":
Having meditated on the three kinds of samādhi,
and having closed the doors of the five paths of affliction,
one sees that what appears as the five paths is conceptual thought.
Conceptual and nonconceptual are both absent;
that nonduality is the genuine path.
In the very state of skillful means and wisdom
one should realize perfect meditative equipoise
and should teach the unsurpassed.

The samaya called "Teaching the Genuine Meaning":
Although inconceivable vehicles are taught,
and although suchness itself does not speak them,
according to the stages of beings' understanding
they are made clear to all, suited to each one.
This is the supreme Vajra Vehicle:
just as water flows down the rivers into the ocean,
and all of it is of the very nature of water,
so all the vehicles are the vajra ground.

The samaya called "Thorough Realization":
For one fully endowed with wisdom and great intellect,
never has it been said that it fails to become the aim.
For the inconceivable beings of the three realms of existence,
the evenness of compassion is inconceivable.
It teaches only what accords with the aim
and cuts the noose of discord, saṃsāra.
It purifies the fields of the sugatas,
and all things gather, pure, into the expanse.

The samaya called "Transforming Awakening":
Having gained mastery over self-aware wisdom,
just as white is changed in color by dye,
and just as poison too becomes medicine,
so the three poisons are awakening,
and the five classes of beings are joined to the path of awakening.
It is the path that frees from affliction and suffering.
If liberated and unliberated are not two,
all things without exception are the cause of awakening.

The samaya called "Equally Perfect, Spontaneously Present":
Wisdom appearing as illusion
and wrong conception appearing as illusion,
since both are illusion, are equal in nature.
Not abiding even in equality itself,
the accumulations of merit and wisdom are perfected.
In the maṇḍala of the two wisdoms,
the vajra of the supreme vehicle is perfected without exception,
with excellent minor marks beyond measure.

The samaya called "Mastery over All":
Through the distinction of thorough realization,
if one aspires, one knows every domain of experience.
Over suchness and over conceptualization
one gains mastery, since they are not two,
and all without exception become fields of bliss,
and all become supreme awakening.
In what has been without entity from the very beginning,
all is accomplished just as it is envisioned.

The samaya called "The Power of the Vidyādhara's Abode":
Having grown familiar with the three kinds of wisdom,
and realizing the meaning of the samādhi of equality,
one should always meditate on the great vastness.
Having gained mastery over self-awareness itself,
in which cause and result are perfected at a single time,
one's age is that of sixteen,
and one remains, prolonging it for hundreds upon hundreds of years,
and without casting off the body one becomes a buddha.

The samaya called "Endowed with an Inexhaustible Treasure":
Through great and utterly pure wisdom,
all domains of experience become pure.
The knower and its domains are not two;
they are of a single taste in suchness.
By joining all things into the field of the Victor,
it becomes an inexhaustible treasure of jewels.
The supreme samaya, endowed with what is inexhaustible,
is Akaniṣṭha, whose edge and center are inconceivable.

The samaya called "Equally Perfect Without Accomplishment":
In the maṇḍala where all is equal,
the suchness in which the expanse and the awareness
of freely moving self-arisen wisdom are not two
is the vajra family.
Where all things are without duality,
there is nothing to accomplish and no accomplishing.
In what is spontaneously present from the very beginning,
nothing can be corrected by a path.

The samaya called "Blessing All":
Although phenomena have been pure from the very beginning,
when the mind is turned around by inversion,
one dwells in wrong conceptions and enjoys them:
the inconceivable deluded saṃsāra of ignorance.
In order to turn back evil, deluded conceptions,
great compassion, through skillful means,
guides the afflicted and places them in the higher realms,
and teaches them to realize the great path of awakening.

The samaya called "Infinite Supreme Speech":
All phenomena without exception
are taught by imputing them in names and words.
However many meanings are expressed,
in vajra speech there is no elaboration.
That which is free of elaboration is the speech of wisdom
that understands every kind of sign in all its variety,
transcending all sounds, names, and words.
All is the lotus free of attachment.

The samaya called "Inexhaustible Transformation":
Through the wisdom of omniscience,
all that appears and exists, without exception,
is mastered by suchness
and dissolved into the expanse of wisdom.
Endowed with an inexhaustible treasure of wisdom,
the blessing of compassion is inconceivable,
and the meanings of the transcendent and the worldly
are each realized and utterly purified.

The samaya called "Understanding All Languages":
For hundreds of thousands of maṇḍalas of liberation,
and for the six classes of beings in all times and places without exception,
though not a single word of speech is uttered,
through the blessing of former compassion,
to both the liberated and the unliberated,
each according to what they wish,
the supreme and sacred speech of the Dharma
urges them toward liberation and purifies the lower realms.

The samaya called "Mastery of Self-Awareness":
In directions and times beyond conception,
all the various signs that are imputed,
however many exist there,
are the conceptualization of self-awareness itself.
In the infinite expanse of buddhafields,
the maṇḍalas of the tathāgatas
and the inconceivable emanations that arise
are a domain purified as emanations of wisdom.

The samaya called "Understanding Without Letters":
Abiding in the inexpressible state
free of letters, words, and sounds,
speech itself is without intrinsic nature,
and all phenomena are pure from the very beginning.
Since all has become the enlightened mind,
it does not abide in sounds and words;
the enlightened mind does not abide in the mind.

The samaya called "Vanquishing the Hosts of Māra":
The afflictions themselves are completely pure;
the five aggregates themselves are manifest buddhas.
Oneself is the maṇḍala of the deities,
the eternal abode free of birth and death.
By the supreme being who has vanquished the four māras,
all the great arrogant ones are vanquished.
The vināyakas of ignorance are tamed and cut off,
and all dissolve into the expanse of phenomena.

The samaya called "Renowned Throughout the Three-Thousandfold World":
Through the blessing of great compassion,
in worlds of the ten directions as numerous as atoms,
the activity for beings is inconceivable.
Through body, speech, mind, qualities,
and spontaneously present wisdom,
no one to be trained is left neglected.
Purifying the lower realms, and in the fields of awakening,
it teaches the Great Perfection of wisdom everywhere.

The samaya called "Supreme Speech of Wisdom":
All things, both transcendent and not transcendent,
when examined thoroughly with correct knowledge,
are without intrinsic nature, yet appear as anything at all.
In their very appearing, they are the expanse itself.
The wisdom that realizes this
has mastery over all and is self-sovereign;
everything appears like the sound of words.
This is the supreme speech of wisdom.

The samaya called "Inconceivable Wisdom, Vajra Speech":
Phenomena are inconceivable;
without edge or center, they pervade everything.
Pervading and not pervading is the vajra speech,
proclaiming various sounds and languages,
the sound of things where there are no things.
Just as when a lute is taken in hand,
although various sounds arise,
there is no thing there: just so is suchness.

The samaya called "Directions and Times Indivisible":
All the ten directions without exception,
and all that arises in the four times without exception,
being without direction and without before and after,
are all the expanse of suchness.
All the separate appearances
of the transmundane and the mundane
are truly gathered in the king, self-awareness;
that self-awareness itself is everything.

The samaya called "Fearless Vajra":
The unconditioned itself is the conditioned;
the conditioned is neither appearing nor non-appearing.
Existence and nonexistence, too, are indivisible.
Its nature pure from the very beginning,
unfabricated and unmade, it is naturally clear.
It cannot be destroyed or demolished:
the vajra self that nothing whatsoever can overcome.
So it was spoken.

The samaya called "The Body of Suchness":
Likewise, in the inconceivable buddhafields
and for all beings without exception,
to the liberated it is a pure body,
to those with concepts it is a body of saṃsāra.
It shows the meaning to each as suits them,
yet the Teacher himself has no substance;
like the moon in water, he appears to all.
Realize everything in this way!

The samaya called "Spontaneously Present Abode of Bliss":
Just as in all phenomena without exception
the heart of awakening is present,
all are gathered in the heart of awakening,
by nature luminous from the very beginning.
Since all things, outer and inner, without exception
abide within one's own state,
oneself has mastery over oneself,
and there is no need to wish for any field of experience.

The samaya called "Great and Supreme, Completely Pure":
Because one has oneself become the king of awareness,
one has mastery over all.
Having taken the unsurpassed ground,
one is the lord of all.
Since all without exception is nondual,
there is no commanding and nothing to be commanded.
Through empowerment in suchness,
all is of one taste in the expanse.

The samaya called "The Maṇḍala of All Phenomena":
In the outer and the inner, the vessel, its contents, and selves,
the natural maṇḍala abides from the very beginning.
All the great appearances of form
are the very maṇḍala of the tathāgatas.
That which holds the displays of illusion
is likewise the maṇḍala of the mothers.
Self-awareness is the maṇḍala of awakening,
abiding as the gathering of all from every side.

The samaya called "Great Inexhaustible Treasure":
The contaminated is like the wealth of yakṣas:
that of sentient beings increases, declines, and is exhausted.
But the qualities of the tathāgatas
are inexhaustible, without increase or decline.
Elaborated into many, they do not dwindle;
gathered into few, they do not grow coarse.
Being an inexhaustible treasure of jewels,
they pervade all things.

The samaya called "Completely Pure Awakening":
Although all phenomena without exception
are completely pure from the very beginning,
through the condition of conceptual thought, adventitiously,
they dwell in the prison of saṃsāra's three realms.
Through the purity of unobscured self-awareness,
when one realizes the meaning, that nirvāṇa and saṃsāra
both are without intrinsic nature,
they are completely pure as a buddhafield.

The samaya called "Rolling Up Apprehended and Apprehender":
When one conceives in a distorted way,
self and mine appear as separate.
Dualistic grasping then enters into that,
and though without intrinsic nature, things appear as separate.
When examined thoroughly through correct realization,
all things are empty.
Not abiding in things or in thingness itself:
realizing that is what frees one from suffering.

The samaya of Dhātvīśvarī, become the Mother:
When there is reliance on the five elements,
all things arise and abide without exception.
It is the ground of all births without exception,
and it is the cause of everything.
Likewise, through the three kinds of wisdom,
diligence free of laziness is called forth.
Likewise, when the expanse itself is realized,
it is the source from which unsurpassed awakening arises.

The samaya called "No Fault in Phenomena":
The unsurpassed maṇḍala
and all the abodes of the lower realms without exception
are both pure in their very cause and nature;
their nature is wisdom, just so.
The world of the completely pure result,
the stable and the moving and the rest,
even as it appears, is without essence.
That itself is the great dharmakāya.

The samaya called "Acting for the Benefit of Sentient Beings":
Since all are one in meaning as the mind of awakening,
great compassion arises as the root.
Through skillful means one practices the perfections,
and travels by way of the afflictions as the path.
Even the afflicted abiding in the three realms
is nothing but distortion appearing.
Realizing it as such is the higher realms and liberation.

The samaya called "Grasping the Meaning Through Realization":
That all is spontaneously present,
and knowing its way to be like an illusion or a mirage:
both are gathered into self-awareness itself.
That itself is declared to be the truth.
What is expressed in sounds and words
draws out the essence of the true meaning.
Without relying on names, words, and sounds,
one should understand it in the depths of the mind.

The samaya called "Dwelling in Mind":
All phenomena without exception, whatever there are,
do not exist apart from mind itself.
Since mind and phenomena are indivisible,
the path of awakening is not far.
It comes from nowhere and goes nowhere;
it is nothing but nonconceptual self-awareness.
Buddhas and sentient beings abide in mind.

The samaya called "Inexpressible, Free of Elaboration":
However many various things are expressed,
whether transmundane or mundane,
apart from their individual characteristics,
not even a hair's breadth is expressed.
Adorned by great merit,
the buddhafield itself is pure.
Through bad actions one dwells in the three realms,
yet that itself is no elaboration apart from the expanse.

The samaya called "Unchanging Vajra Speech":
The emanations of pure body and speech,
and the various displays of the Great and Supreme as well,
appear to the mind of awakening,
and in their very appearing they are gathered into the expanse.
Through inconceivable distorted movements,
one circles in the abodes of the three realms and the five paths.
That itself is wrong understanding appearing in various ways;
apart from that there is nothing at all.

The samaya called "Unfabricated from the Very Beginning":
Awakening beyond intellect need not be wished for.
All the five kinds of beings without exception
abide in the heart of awakening from the very beginning.
The expanse of suchness pervades everything.
Into this state of things as they are,
the mind that realizes it thus enters,
and when it is realized as nondual,
who is it that fabricates, and who that realizes?

The samaya called "Natural Luminosity":
All phenomena without exception,
as self-awareness pure from the very beginning,
are naturally clear, fabricated by no one.
That domain of those who possess wisdom,
unattached like a lotus,
is not something common to all.
That domain of the tathāgatas
is realized by persons of supreme acuity of mind.

The samaya called "Dhātvīśvarī of All":
Become a treasure of inexhaustible qualities,
a wish-fulfilling treasury that knows no time of exhaustion,
it arises from the most profound mind,
free of self and of mine.
When the meaning of the profound dharmadhātu is realized,
the bodies and wisdoms are inconceivable.
When the meaning of self-aware wisdom is realized,
one attains the eternal state free of birth and death.

The samaya called "Binding to Awakening":
When, with wisdom free of apprehended and apprehender,
one rests in equipoise in the expanse without attachment,
all is the field of suchness,
and affliction and suffering will not arise.
Having myself perfectly completed it,
in accord with the nature of phenomena just as it is,
I shall establish the lesser vehicle and the distorted,
all of them, in the expanse of equality.

The samaya called "Directions and Times Nondual":
All the ten directions and the four times
appear separately as marks of understanding,
yet in their very appearing they are indivisible.
Like a sphere without edge or center,
self-awareness, too,
is without edge or center and is not many.
It goes nowhere, yet pervades everything;
granting bliss to all, it abides nowhere.

The samaya called "All Endowed with Riches":
When phenomena are understood individually
and all is known as the nondual expanse,
one attains the ornament endowed with inexhaustibility,
an ever-inexhaustible treasure of jewels.
When one takes up residence in suchness,
affliction and suffering do not arise;
it is awakening itself, ever abiding in bliss,
endowed with all the major and minor marks.

The samaya called "The Two, Skillful Means and Wisdom":
All that appears, in whatever way,
is all of the nature of skillful means.
Means is joined to means,
yet joining and the joined both do not exist.
All the various appearances,
even as they appear, are without intrinsic nature.
That itself is declared to be wisdom.
Means itself is never apprehended.

The samaya called "Joining Wisdom Itself as Means":
From the very expanse of suchness
various displays arise as anything at all.
In their very arising they are without intrinsic nature;
in their very nonexistence the various appear.
Means and wisdom arise from the expanse.
With the wisdom of hearing, reflecting, and meditating,
realize the meaning of wisdom as means!
In this way bring everything to its wealth!

The samaya called "The Nondual Mind of Awakening":
When means and wisdom are united,
when the nondual mind of awakening radiates,
it is the source and the maker of all;
there is nothing at all that it has not made.
It is not any color, shape, or kind;
extremely hard to examine, no one knows it.
If even I myself do not see myself,
how could another realize it?

The samaya called "Uniting All":
From the very beginning they are gods and goddesses:
the true union of the maṇḍala,
and the joining of the faculties with their objects.
The five aggregates themselves are the tathāgatas;
the five elements themselves are joined as the mothers,
joined as the very goddesses of the four times;
eternalism and nihilism, the four alternatives, are the wrathful goddesses.

All things without exception, all the elements,
are joined with the expanse of equality and with awareness.
Or else, with the wisdom consort, the woman of the expanse,
arrange the entire maṇḍala of the Sugata,
meditate on yourself as the deity of awareness,
and unite in nonduality.
When vajra and bell are joined,
the lotus of awakening is greatly delighted.
The maṇḍalas of the directions and times are pleased,
and many beings attain bliss;
then dissolve once more into the nondual expanse.
Having dissolved, blaze forth from the expanse as the mudrā.
The skillful means and wisdom of the Sugata are perfected;
the six mudrās, too, are fully perfected;
the two great accumulations of merit are also perfected.
Since all is fulfilled in the expanse,
one does not fall into the lower realms, and true accomplishment is gained.
This is the great liberation.
Liberating itself is union;
union itself liberates all without exception.

The samaya called "Liberating All Without Exception":
All sentient beings without exception
who dwell in however many outer and inner vessels and contents
should be liberated into the expanse
through realization of the great self.
Just as the supremely great Wrathful One
liberated the Great God through skillful means,
so, through the samādhi that realizes the nature of reality,
liberate them nondually through skillful means.
Through the blessing of great compassion,
summon all the beings of the five paths
and liberate them all without exception.
All beings without exception
who dwell in the four continents of great Mount Meru,
blessing them with great compassion,
when you meditate on the great fire of wisdom,
should all be burned without exception.
Dissolve them again into the expanse,
bless them as tathāgatas,
and empower them as heirs of the Victors.
Although all this liberation is performed,
there is no thing at all that is liberated.
When the five aggregates are destroyed,
once destroyed, the elements become latent;
even when they are not destroyed, they are elements.
If one kills, consciousness being without existence,
even as it abides, it has no substance.
As for that nature of mind,
even if the buddhas of the three times rose up as enemies,
no one could seize it, and there is no slaying it.
Then what is it that compassion liberates?
All conceptual signs
are turned back from distortion,
and through samādhi, mantra, and mudrā,
and through thorough habituation, they are empowered
and established in the field of awakening.
Just that is designated "liberation."
Liberation itself is union.
Having cut affliction and suffering at the root,
since one liberates into the abode of awakening,
it is union in the nondual expanse.
Skillful means itself is wisdom, and wisdom itself is skillful means;
nonduality itself is the mind of awakening.

Union is the maṇḍala of union as gods and goddesses; the joining of the faculties, objects, and times; the joining of the five great ones as the five tathāgatas; the joining as the mothers of what exists merely as the illusory display of each; the joining of the faculties, objects, and times as gods and goddesses; the joining of eternalism, nihilism, and the four alternatives as wrathful gods and wrathful goddesses; and joining in direct perception.

Liberation is liberating through skillful means; liberating through transference; liberating into the equality of the four times; liberating all without exception into the expanse at a single moment; and liberating all nondually through great compassion.

These two are performed at the same time; they should not be allowed to become separate. "The supreme accomplishment, too, is attained there": so the Tathāgata himself explained to the Tathāgata himself.

The samaya called "Taking What Is Not Given":
All phenomena without exception,
whether transmundane or mundane,
are nothing other than self and mine,
yet by their own nature they are selfless.
Although one enjoys everything without obstruction,
the enjoyed and the enjoying both do not exist.
Since all is empty from the very beginning,
who is it that takes, and what is enjoyed?
Although one enjoys without obstruction
all of appearance and existence without exception,
one is not bound: it is an accumulation of merit,
it is great wisdom free of attachment.
All without exception is the expanse of phenomena;
thus it is declared to be self-awareness.
When it is realized as nondual,
the very taking is Samantabhadra.

The samaya called "Speaking Falsehood":
All phenomena without exception
have never existed as things from the very beginning.
By nature they are naturally luminous,
and although they are indicated by mere names and words,
names and words have no substance.
Through the deluded concepts of ignorance,
where there is no "I" and no self,
separate signs are grasped as things;
yet there is no grasping thing at all.
All phenomena are mere names and words.
However much is said,
there is no speaker and nothing spoken.
All phenomena appear as false;
in falsehood itself one engages in falsehood.
What is called "false" is a mere designation;
all are words of falsehood.

The samaya called "Vajra Destroyer":
From beginningless time, in a distorted way,
where there is no self and no mine,
conceptual signs are grasped as separate,
yet the thing that is grasped does not exist.
In order to destroy discordant signs,
with vajra-like samādhi
destroy all the habitual tendencies of self-conception,
and dissolve into the vajra expanse.

The samaya called "The Wheel of Dharma":
Through the blessing of great compassion:
the afflicted beings who circle in the three realms
do not circle because another has bound them;
they circle through their own grasping, concepts, and attachment.
You who are richly endowed with great intelligence,
listen to the supreme meaning of the Dharma:
saṃsāra, without being abandoned, is the buddhafield.
Realize all without exception as the nature of reality.

The samaya called "Source of Jewels":
The supreme wealth is the seven treasures;
they are not found by seeking them with desire.
When, beyond body, speech, and mind, unobjectifying,
in a manner free of attachment,
one is endowed with them nondually,
the seven great treasures will arise,
endowed with the various major and minor marks.

The samaya called "Unattached Lotus":
In all phenomena without exception,
whether transmundane or mundane,
there is no staining and nothing to be stained,
like a lotus born from the mud.
Of all the limitless appearances, however they appear,
not a single one exists apart from oneself.
Since oneself is utterly unobservable,
even the afflictions themselves are the field of awakening.

The samaya called "Accomplishing All Aims":
The vajra family vanquishes all;
the jewel family brings forth qualities;
the lotus family becomes free of attachment.
Body, speech, mind, and the five wisdoms
fulfill all aims.
Abandoning apprehended and apprehender,
the mind gazes at the expanse of equality.
Free of birth and death, spontaneously present,
the body, speech, mind, qualities,
and wondrous activity renowned to all
do not exist anywhere else:
they are self-arisen wisdom without location.
In the manner of seeing, the appearances of form
and the appearance as the body of unsurpassed awakening,
just as in a mirror,
appear as various colors and shapes.
The speech of the tathāgatas
transcends all sounds, names, and words;
its inexpressible nature is understood everywhere.
Free of affliction, the unsurpassed sound,
unuttered, resounds through the three-thousandfold world;
the speech it utters is inconceivable.
In the very uttering, there is no three-thousandfold world;
that itself utters the various.
Endowed with the omniscient mind,
examined thoroughly through true realization:
the expanse without ground or root, momentary,
whose edge and center cannot be found by examination,
that itself is the cause from which the various arise,
unfabricated and unmade by anyone.
Even I myself do not observe myself;
insubstantial, it has mastery over all.
When one is truly endowed with body and speech,
the qualities are equal to the limits of space,
gathered into the unchanging buddhafield.
In all fields of experience without exception,
things arise just as they are intended.
The great inexhaustible treasure is accomplished:
the precious wealth of awakening
that no one else can steal.
Through the blessing of great compassion,
the four kinds of activity are performed without act or agent;
there is no doing and nothing to be done.
The qualities of the buddhas, spontaneously present from the very beginning,
engage with everything in the manner of an illusion.
In the maṇḍala of the state free of engagement,
they play in a manner free of attachment.
Though acts and agents are of various kinds,
in their very doing they are like the moon in water.
The wisdom free of apprehended and apprehender
is spoken of as "the five roots,"
yet in the very speaking it does not exist at all;
without characteristics, the various appear.
That itself is omniscience itself.
Pervading all and abiding nowhere,
it is awareness become sovereign,
inconceivable and all-pervading.

The samaya called "Illuminating Space":
All phenomena without exception
are pervaded throughout by suchness.
All vessels and contents without exception
are pervaded by great light.
Just as space cannot be grasped,
all appearance and existence is like that.
All phenomena are mind,
and mind is of the nature of space.

The samaya called "The Great Earth, the Support":
Just as on the great earth
all births without exception arise,
both those held by mind and those not held by mind,
and it is the abode and support of all,
so in the unsurpassed Great Vehicle
all the awakened buddhas abide;
all the vehicles, high and low, arise from it,
and all are gathered into the expanse of the Great Vehicle.

The samaya called "The Burning Fire of Wisdom":
Through selfless wisdom, self-awareness,
the habitual tendencies of things and non-things are burned.
When particular and general phenomena
and all the jealousies of the mind
are realized to be without substance,
saṃsāra is completely pure without being abandoned.
One does not pass into nirvāṇa; one abides there from the very beginning.
There is no going and no abiding to be done.
All concepts are burned up from the very beginning.

The samaya called "Washing All":
All sentient beings without exception
circle in saṃsāra because of the two obscurations.
When, through the wisdom of hearing, reflecting, and meditating,
the two kinds of selflessness are realized,
the perfections are completed and the ten grounds accomplished;
having perfectly completed the three grounds,
one establishes all in the field of equality.
This is the supreme ablution.

The samaya called "The Great Scatterer":
Sentient beings, pure from the very beginning,
become covered by self-grasping like countless motes of dust.
By realizing that mind and the dust do not exist,
the wind of wisdom scatters the habitual tendencies.
Phenomena are from the very beginning like the wind;
just as it cannot be grasped,
in that way bring everything to fulfillment.
Unfabricated, it abides thus from the very beginning.

The samaya called "Mastery over All":
Through undistorted self-awareness
that realizes the nonduality of apprehended and apprehender,
when all becomes the state of equality,
since all without exception is oneself,
one has mastery over all,
and whatever one intends is accomplished.
However one acts, it is an accumulation of merit;
it is the great wisdom of equal awareness.

The samaya called "Knowing All Phenomena as Reflections":
It is the source from which all arises;
in their very appearing, like a rainbow,
all things appear without intrinsic nature.
That itself is declared to be the dharmakāya,
stained by nothing at all and abandoning nothing.

Forms that appear separately and in accord,
not imputed as "I" or as self,
appear within such a state.

The samaya called "Knowing Phenomena to Be Like Sound":
However many sounds are uttered,
from what is no thing comes the sound of things;
utterance and sound are not things.
Know phenomena to be like sound!
All the meanings that arise without exception
do not exist apart from what words indicate.
Sound itself is not the thing that is meant,
yet meaning itself does not exist apart from sound.

The samaya called "Knowing Phenomena to Have the Character of Scent":
All phenomena, without substance,
arise in various ways and spread forth.
Likewise, the thing that is scent
does not exist, yet it is smelled everywhere.
With wisdom free of doubt,
know all phenomena in this way!
Although one is fully satisfied by scent,
scent and thing are not two.
All accomplishments are like that.

The samaya called "Knowing All Phenomena to Be Like Taste":
All phenomena without exception
arise from what has neither ground nor root.
Likewise, when the insubstantial faculty,
the substance that has no entity,
and consciousness, faculty, and object come together,
the faculty of taste is fully accomplished.
Taste, and the realizing of it as taste,
are momentary and without intrinsic nature.

The samaya called "Knowing All Phenomena to Be Like Touch":
Forms are like masses of foam;
feelings are like bubbles on water;
grasping at signs is like a mirage.
When consciousness is governed by illusion,
various signs are certain to arise.
One experiences every supreme taste of bliss as well.
That itself is a great accumulation of merit;
bliss is wisdom free of attachment.
The accomplishment of touch is like that.

The samaya called "Conditioned Things Completely Pure":
When one takes up residence in the true itself,
the outer and inner, vessel and contents, are completely pure.
What appears as coarse is the tathāgatas;
what holds it as an illusion is the expanse of the Mother.
Form is a sign without substance.
When each is broken down into its fives,
all abide in the state of space,
and space, likewise, is the expanse of phenomena.
This is the accomplishment of the conditioned itself.

The samaya called "Conditioned Things Arising Everywhere":
The bodies of the sugatas appearing as illusion,
and wrong understanding appearing as illusion:
both are without ground or root, empty of intrinsic nature,
and cannot be analyzed into families or lineages.
Because they have no family, color, or shape,
the outer and inner, vessel and contents, appear.
In their very appearing they are the dharmakāya;
they become anything at all and accomplish everything.
The accomplishment of arising everywhere is like that.

The samaya called "Overpowering the Majesty of the Elephant":
All sentient beings without exception
are distracted by many concepts and formations.
Like a child whose strength has not unfolded,
they remain distracted by many acts and agents.
Since the many acts and agents are selfless,
when one masters them through samādhi,
signs abide in the expanse of equality,
and the various formations are unmoving and stable.

The samaya called "Perfecting the Five Powers":
Since all phenomena are unborn,
one attains the power of utter stability.
Since self-aware wisdom is luminous,
one also attains the power of omniscience.
Since all phenomena are indivisible,
one attains the power to enjoy everything,
the way of enjoying without attachment.
All, too, are by nature complete from the very beginning.

The samaya called "Sealing All":
The four directions, the four intermediate directions, above and below, all ten,
are pervaded throughout by the great nature.
That self-arisen itself, being without substance,
is the illusory seal of all.
That profound dharmadhātu itself
abides in oneself and is hard to examine.
All are gathered into the expanse of equality,
into the field that abides in bliss from the very beginning.

The samaya called "Phenomena Indivisible":
Since all are of one taste in the expanse from the very beginning,
"I" and self are not found there.
In what is by nature completely pure,
there is not even the thought of clean and unclean.
Since all is the essence of the Sugata,
how could the dust of conditions stain it?
Realize it thus with supreme wisdom;
know the expanse in which all without exception is gathered!

The samaya called "Terrifier of All":
One endowed with wisdom and acuity of mind,
having demolished all the signs of analysis and conception,
truly realizes that all are gathered
in the seal of signlessness.
Concept itself is without substance,
and that itself appears as the various;
in its very appearing there is nothing to be purified.
That itself is the seal of destruction.

The samaya called "Eternal Increase of Life":
Since all phenomena are oneself,
where would one go, and where would one be born?
There is no thing that perishes or is born;
in what has no substance, who is there to kill?
Whoever grasps dualistically,
for him there are birth and death.
When it is examined by one who possesses wisdom,
in birth and destruction themselves there is no substance.

The samaya called "Obstructing Nothing":
From the perfection of the three supreme ones downward
to the abodes of the lower realms upward,
all are truly indivisible;
nothing whatsoever is to be abandoned.
Of the inconceivable vehicles, high and low,
none at all was taught to be without purpose.
All the abodes of the lower realms without exception
possess the cause of unsurpassed awakening.

The samaya called "Enjoying Everything":
All is Samantabhadra;
the five qualities of sense pleasure
are to be enjoyed without hindrance
and regarded as a great accumulation of merit.
Regard them also as the five Tathāgatas.
Furthermore, all the thousandfold world-systems,
the expanse of wisdom, and the sense pleasures,
the best, the middling and the least, are all to be enjoyed.

The samaya called "Not Handing It to All":
In the maṇḍala where directions and times are equal,
one who has not realized the supreme vajra family,
one of the lesser vehicle and lesser view,
will not realize the supreme vehicle.
Though the meaning of the unequalled pervades everything,
they are obscured by affliction and suffering,
and so they do not see the truth itself.
To those who do not see, it is not to be handed over.

The samaya called "Nothing to Guard":
For all the buddhas without exception
there is nothing to accomplish and nothing to guard.
There is neither virtue nor nonvirtue,
no abandoning of afflictions and no accomplishing of purity.
When one has become the expanse of suchness,
pleasure and displeasure are indivisible,
abiding as primordially pure equality.
In that there is not even an atom to guard.

The samaya called "Guarding Nothing":
Sentient beings of the three realms of saṃsāra,
circling since beginningless time,
act in confusion amid the inverted views,
and cling intensely to the objects of apprehended and apprehender;
therefore they wander forever in saṃsāra.
So, realizing your own mind,
abandon all this completely, without laziness,
and guard equality, without "I" and without self.

The samaya called "Terrifying All":
If all the roots of the vows
decreed by Vajrasattva
are not guarded as their meaning requires,
the wrathful yakṣas, blazing like fire,
will tear out the heart and drink the blood,
and one will be born in the three lower realms.
Its suffering is beyond bearing.
This samaya is the great vajra.

The samaya called "Enjoying the Sacred Substances Individually":
In the division into the five families, each in turn:
for Vajrasattva, the flesh of an elephant;
for the Jewel, the flesh of a horse;
for the Lotus family, the flesh of the arika;
for Amogha, the flesh of a garuḍa;
for the Sugata family, the great flesh.
Whether divided or undivided,
they are certainly to be accepted.
Or else gather them all into one;
or else regard them as without characteristics.

The samaya of the mudrā of samādhi:
All the maṇḍalas of the Sugata without exception,
luminous by nature from the very beginning,
are said to be present in everything.
The maṇḍala of the wisdom being itself
is the unsurpassed and supreme;
by all of the supreme vajra family
it is to be practised constantly, without interruption.
One will be born at the foot of the bodhi tree,
and unsurpassed, supreme awakening will be accomplished.
The lotus beyond birth and death is accomplished,
and mastery over mind will also be attained.
Whatever sphere of activity one aspires to, one will know.

This is the seventy-sixth chapter, on the elaboration of the samayas, from the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion.

Whoever abandons this maṇḍala
is born, through affliction, in the abodes of the lower realms.
He will not attain the fruit of awakening;
the cause of the unsurpassed destiny is cut off,
and he will be born as one whose lineage is severed.
One who wishes to accomplish Vajrasattva,
constantly realizing the meaning of equality,
should meditate on the body as like a rainbow,
appearing, yet without intrinsic nature.
Knowing the retinue to be like the moon in water,
meditate on them as clear, yet without intrinsic nature.
If one meditates clearly on the bodies of the deities,
one will gain a great accumulation of merit.
If one knows them to be like reflections,
one will gain a great accumulation of wisdom.
The yogin should meditate always.
If one abandons that yidam deity,
one will always experience the misery of the lower realms.

The meaning of not interrupting the continuity of mantra and mudrā:
Mantra and mudrā are not to be interrupted.
This is the path by which yoga is accomplished;
it is not letting go, it is restraint, and it is guidance;
it is the abode where the supreme goal is perfected.
Constant meditative equipoise is the mudrā;
the mantra is what uproots the three poisons.
One who longs for the great river of the true meaning
should, at all times without break, with mudrā and mantra,
recite intensely with a steady mind.
Its qualities are equal to the bounds of space:
the moving of the mudrā and the syllable oṃ.

The characteristics of the vajra master are taught:
The teacher of the unsurpassed maṇḍala
is the giver of inexhaustible qualities,
the guide on the great path of awakening,
who places beings in the realm free of attachment.
He cuts affliction and suffering at the root
and empties the paths of saṃsāra.
He is the lamp that dispels the darkness of ignorance,
the guide of the blind.
If one constantly makes offerings to him,
the awakening of a buddha is not far off.

The characteristics of vajra brothers and sisters are taught:
They are siblings of the vajra family:
through karma held in common since beginningless time,
through a common view and conduct, they are born here.
Each is to the other like one's own eyes;
cherish them deeply and love them.
If, with ill will against the samaya, one finds fault with them,
one will fall into the great hell.
Therefore do not transgress the samaya.

The secret samaya is taught:
The maṇḍala of the Tathāgatas
pervades all of the three realms of existence without exception;
but being thoroughly obscured by inverted views,
it is like the pauper's treasure and the wrestler's jewel.
Therefore this supreme vajra secret,
through the compassion of the Omniscient One,
not because there is any fault in the Dharma,
is kept utterly secret for the sake of beings.
If the secret meaning is shown
to those of the lesser vehicle and those of inverted views,
not knowing the secret meaning of the true nature,
they take those immediate spheres of conduct
to be the absolutely definitive meaning,
and lead that very secret meaning astray.
Not realizing it, they circle in hell.
Therefore keep it utterly secret.
Endowed with great compassion,
be skilled in all the paths to liberation.
Since you are endowed with supreme wisdom,
according to the grades of beings as vessels,
the meaning of the higher and lower vehicles
must be taught to each as befits them.

Accepting and not abandoning
the yogin should always practise.
Becoming free of attachment,
and the great and marvellous accomplishments,
are found within the summary teaching on samaya.
If one practises without realization,
one is born among pretas and rākṣasas.
Or else, with samaya such as this,
however one acts, one does not transgress the vow,
and deeds and conduct become the two great accumulations.
Whether going, lying down or sitting,
abide in the expanse of that equality.
Act always with supreme wisdom.
Toward all phenomena without exception
act as a bird cuts across the path of the sky.
All grasping and clinging without exception
are like a great wind streaming through space;
all the mind's conceptual attachments without exception
are like a great fish darting through water.
Look with the yogic view in that way.
With wisdom that realizes in this way,
not mere words, deepen your understanding.

Those who boast of the supreme Vajra Vehicle
without knowing the meaning of equality,
saying, "The samaya of secret mantra is equality,"
and act just as they please,
will pass on through the hell realms.

What is the meaning of equality?
There is neither equality nor realization at all.
Abiding in nothing, one engages objects;
there is no one who acts and no action.
The supreme of the family who realizes thus,
when born as a vajra vidyādhara,
is fearlessly joined with Samantabhadra
and abides in inexhaustible qualities.

Not knowing the meaning of equality,
yet with the relative and the ultimate not two,
and with phenomena and persons both without self:
this is the stage of the bodhisattva.
Not knowing all karma and affliction
as the meaning of equality,
and holding dependent origination, cause and effect, to exist:
this is the pratyekabuddha.
To view, in all phenomena without exception,
the things of the four truths as existent,
and so to come to abandon and not abandon:
this is the stage of the śrāvaka.
Taking the ten virtues as chief,
and virtue and nonvirtue as unequal,
leads to birth as gods and humans:
the stage of circling in the desire realm.
Not realizing the meaning of equality,
grasping in sequence at self, each in turn,
in the four immeasurables and the four meditative concentrations,
and conceiving the aggregate of form as self:
this is birth, in sequence, in the abodes of Brahmā.
Not realizing equality and formlessness,
not knowing the four name aggregates to be nonexistent,
and so coming to abide in the four sense sources,
one is born on the level of the Peak of Existence.
If one does not know all to be equal,
hates all without exception,
and comes to lack true compassion,
this is dwelling in the sixteen hell realms
of the great unbearable torments.
Not knowing the nature free of attachment,
desiring everything whatsoever,
obscured by the body and obscured by food,
one is born in the realm of the great pretas.
Not knowing that there is no "I" and no self,
being deluded about all phenomena,
with wisdom unclear and to no purpose,
not realizing the meaning of virtue and evil:
this is birth in the realm of animals.
Giving rise to very great pride,
taking everything as one's own,
and engaging in strife with all,
one is born in the realm of the asuras.

All the vehicles, supreme, high and low,
and all the views of the world without exception,
when thoroughly examined by great wisdom,
are without intrinsic nature, yet anything at all is accomplished.
There is no falling from the expanse of the true nature.
One endowed with the eye of great wisdom
sees the high and low states and the six paths,
without intrinsic nature, yet anything at all accomplished,
each likewise as saṃsāra.

Through the unsurpassed supreme mantra,
without examining any signs at all,
all signs and everything are absent.
Since it is equality with everything,
the separate views of the transmundane and the mundane
are not to be spoken of.
In general, the inconceivable wrong conceptions
do not exist from the very beginning;
through the habitual tendencies of individual conceptions
one is obscured by the net of apprehended and apprehender.
Nothing at all, it appears as anything at all;
in its very appearing there is no intrinsic nature.
To realize it thus is the abode of the happy destinies.
The Vajra Holder realizes it thus.

The view and conduct of great yoga:
however one acts, there is no intrinsic nature.
Like a great bird soaring in the sky,
like a great fish darting through water,
like a great wind moving through space,
look with the yogic view in that way.
The great conduct of yoga
is to act toward everything without distinction.
One comes to taste the supreme flavour of bliss.
Who is there to cling to it?
Acting yet unattached, that is wisdom;
the very supreme of bliss is the accumulation of merit.
Act within such a state:
this is the vast conduct.
Without letting view and conduct part from each other,
mantra holders should always practise.
Some, though their view abides in equality,
are not at all in harmony with conduct.
Some, though they perfect conduct,
do not know the view of equality at all.
Some have neither view nor conduct.
The truly supreme and holy being,
since ultimate and relative are indivisible,
realizes them thoroughly just as they are.

Abiding in the state of the unsurpassed Great Vehicle,
ultimate and relative are indivisible,
yet toward all things, merely on the relative level,
one holds both pure and impure.
In the great vehicle of means,
purification and suffering
are indivisible even on the relative level:
such is the whole difference of higher and lower views.
The great view and conduct of means
surpasses the unsurpassed vehicle:
ultimately it is the seven riches;
relatively it is the perfection of equality.

While not moving from equality,
one displays the great mudrā of means.
Means and wisdom are completely pure.
What is called "all is pure"
does not mean that things are plainly pure;
in appearance and conduct themselves they are completely pure.

In the sūtras of the great samaya,
the teaching of the five families is this:
all phenomena without exception
are entirely gathered into the great families.
All phenomena without exception are not perceived as separate;
unshakeable by anything, it is vajra;
because it subdues the base families, it is vajra.
All arising from the unperceivable expanse,
it is the abode of great qualities
and the abode of the overwhelming buddha-fields as well:
this is declared to be the Jewel family.
Because all phenomena are indivisible,
there is no attachment and nothing to be attached to;
they are one in suchness,
untainted by faults, like a lotus.
Because all without exception is gathered into the expanse,
here there is not the slightest thing to be done.
Without acting, aims are accomplished,
and faults and qualities are joined as awakening itself:
this is the accomplishment of actions.
All phenomena without exception
are free of extremes and centre, free of multiplicity.
Not being anything at all, they are suchness;
realizing the mode of indivisibility,
this is the family of the Tathāgata.

For all of appearance and existence without exception
it makes a place of clarity and warmth,
and burns up signs and habitual tendencies:
this is called the family of fire.
All phenomena without exception
appear as things, yet there is nothing to grasp;
when it stirs, all things speak:
this is called the great wind.
In the sūtras of the great samaya,
the five elements are explained as
the source from which all without exception arises,
the treasury that makes all things grow.
For all phenomena without exception,
since there is a ground that is solid and stable,
yet one realizes the mode of no things,
this is called the great earth.
For all phenomena without exception,
likewise the great space
opens room for outer and inner;
it is the very state in which all is clear.
And all four elements, every one,
dissolve into the state of space.
All phenomena without exception
are gathered into the expanse of suchness.

The samaya called "The Five Afflictions":
Ignorance becomes awareness;
the inverted is the true;
the five afflictions are the Tathāgatas.
If their insubstantiality is realized, they are the path of awakening;
if not realized, one goes to the three lower realms.
Therefore, by hearing, reflecting and meditating,
realize the true meaning itself,
and join everything as buddhahood.
Since desire has no intrinsic nature,
be attached to the true meaning,
abiding in the manner of non-attachment.
Attachment to non-attachment
is the great, supreme, utter attachment.
The realms of the Victors will arise:
these are the pure fields of Amitābha,
where all are miraculously born from lotuses,
and one is born on the level of Vajradhara.
The characteristic of great aversion:
it is aversion to the inverted.
If one knows aversion to be without substance,
one knows hating and not hating as equal:
unshakeable by anything, it is vajra.
The state of great equality
is indivisible in all things;
not realizing it so, it appears to everyone as different.
Likewise ordinary people do not know equality,
and so they see it as mere aversion.
When thoroughly examined with true realization,
aversion is great compassion.
As for what is called delusion and bewilderment:
all phenomena without exception
are indivisible on the ultimate level,
and on the relative level are like an illusion or a mirage.
Since in their very appearing they have no substance,
who would grasp at them as a self?
Not perceiving anything, all being indivisible:
that is the delusion of the wise.
Whoever realizes it is a Tathāgata.
The characteristic of pride is stated thus:
having settled in equality,
toward the inverted and the lesser vehicle
it appears as very great pride.
Pride and peace are not separate;
being without intrinsic nature, they are gathered into the expanse.
This is the source of qualities.
Know it likewise to be pure.
The characteristic of jealousy is stated thus:
it is grasping at "I" and self,
yet the grasping itself has no substance.
Jealous toward those who do not know equality,
it gathers all into equality,
and is strictly restrained toward what is unequal:
therefore it is the great jealousy.
Bound as a self through the inverted,
one is made utterly narrow by apprehended and apprehender.
The wisdom free of apprehended and apprehender
has neither narrow nor wide; all is equal.
By knowing that, actions are fully accomplished.

The samaya called "The Five Aggregates":
Though appearing as the five aggregates,
they perform the various activities of the relative,
and experience objects of pleasure and objects of displeasure,
each with its own flavours.
When the characteristics of the five are taken apart,
their individual characteristics have no substance.
Whoever grasps them as substantial
will be born in the three lower realms.
Here, the characteristic of form:
its very appearing is without intrinsic nature;
non-appearance itself displays variety;
appearance and non-appearance are not two.
To realize this is the abode of liberation;
form itself is the body of perfect enjoyment.
The characteristic of feeling is stated thus:
though three kinds arise in everyone,
pleasure and displeasure have no substance,
and what is neither is the indeterminate.
Though various feelings arise,
both feeling and arising are empty;
emptiness is not a substance.
To realize this is to don the armour of the Dharma.
The characteristic of perception is explained thus:
it grasps the meeting of the three as signs.
When its individual characteristics are taken apart,
there is no substance; it is suchness.
That substance is manifold,
and without any agent it engages everywhere.
In all of the three realms without exception,
signs great and small are beyond conception;
pervasive and without substance, they do not exist as things.
If one realizes this, these are the marks of a buddha.
The characteristic of formations stands thus:
their number grows from eight roots
and moves through all as fifty-one.
Realized there, no root exists;
though they do not exist, false movements arise.
Whoever grasps them as substantial,
forever distracted by afflictions,
circles all through the prison of the three realms.
For the sake of the true meaning, through awareness,
the ground and root of their spreading is suchness itself.
Then, the arising of consciousness
is without substance and momentary;
it plays in the expanse of wisdom,
and is said to be the great host of emanations.
Consciousness is the source of all.
The nature of its substance
is not colour, shape or a stream of knowing;
it is not anything at all, itself unperceivable.
This holds sway over all:
going to the higher realms or going to the lower realms
is the difference between realizing and not realizing.

If one knows the four within the state of equality,
this is great wisdom.

The samaya of the five colours:
Though all sentient beings without exception
are pervaded by the five wisdoms,
they are obscured by their own conceptions
and do not see the meaning of the five wisdoms.
For all who do not see and are deluded,
in order to show the characteristics of wisdom,
white and red and yellow,
green and the rest, and black, are shown.
Having realized the selflessness of phenomena,
because the mind of awakening itself is pure,
the colour shown is utterly white:
it is the colour of purity, so that this may be realized.
All phenomena without exception
are called padmarāga, the ruby;
there is no attachment and nothing to be attached to,
and, because it is free of attachment, it is red.
A great treasure of qualities that knows no exhaustion,
it is the source from which all arises;
in the whole expanse of suchness
it becomes an inexhaustible treasure of jewels:
its colour is shown as the colour of gold.
For phenomena that are inverted,
through the blessing of great compassion,
they must be truly gathered into the expanse,
and so all actions without exception are brought to completion:
all-encompassing action is the colour green.
From the very expanse of suchness,
unshakeable by anything and unfabricated,
the substance of the colour black
cannot be altered by other dyes.
Know all things likewise.

The samaya called "The Five Directions":
Though likewise there is no edge and no centre,
for one abiding in the state of indivisibility,
for the sake of realizing wisdom,
one should abide in the directions and the centre.
For sentient beings blinded by ignorance,
in order to clear away affliction and ignorance,
the lamp of wisdom, blazing with light,
dispels the darkness of ignorance;
therefore it is called the east, where light rises.
For all sentient beings without exception,
affliction and suffering do not remain,
and the darkness of ignorance sets;
therefore it is called the direction of the west, where light sets.
All the afflictions of all sentient beings,
through great wisdom, which is selflessness,
are thoroughly purified of suffering;
therefore it is called the direction of the north, the pure.
Thus for all without exception,
compassion rises and obscurations set.
When the characteristics of conception have come forth in this way,
their great qualities are complete.
Thus all phenomena without exception
