are gathered into the expanse of the nature itself.
All the directions without exception
are round, in the manner of suchness.
Look upon all the directions in that way.
If, through ignorance and the inverted views,
the directions are grasped as separate,
one will forever circle in afflicted saṃsāra.

The samaya called "The Five Faculties":
Exercising power over the five objects,
the inverted grasp them as things;
by grasping them as things, they circle in the three realms.
All forms without exception
appear yet are without substance,
and are imagined as substantial gods and goddesses;
self and deity are then two.
Though sounds resound in all their variety,
know sound itself to be without substance;
all sounds and speech without exception
are the various great sounds of the Dharma.
Though appearing as the substance of smell,
smell itself has no nature whatsoever;
smelling is the scent of the Dharma:
great wisdom realizes it so.
Though appearing as the substance of taste,
taste has no intrinsic nature;
tasting is the taste of suchness,
and that bliss brings satisfaction.
Though various tangibles appear,
there is no existing substance of the tangible;
what appears as tangible is enjoyment;
great bliss is the Dharma body.
If those who do not know, the inverted,
become attached to the objects of the faculties,
they are never parted from desire,
and in the three lower realms and the eight unfree states,
like fire in roots and straw,
they will circle through the three realms.
Therefore realize wisdom free of attachment.
Make merit without hoarding what has been done.
Acting in all things and remaining unstained,
always meditate on great wisdom.

The samaya called "The Five Equalities": equality as unborn on the ultimate level; equality as illusion on the relative level; equality as the indivisibility of the ultimate and the relative; equality of the five great elements as the five Tathāgatas; and equality of the eight consciousnesses as the five wisdoms.

Of these, equality as unborn on the ultimate level:
All phenomena without exception
are suchness, the limit of emptiness,
the true, and the dharmadhātu.
They are without characteristics and unperceivable,
ungraspable, and with nothing to be cured;
they are in nirvāṇa from the very beginning.
All have the characteristic of space,
and space too has no characteristic.

Equality as illusion on the relative level:
The various conditioned things
all appear in the manner of an illusion;
they perform all the activities of the moment,
and the signs of every realization appear.
All of these are reflections:
agent and action appear, yet have no substance.
All abide like the moon in water;
all appear, yet are without intrinsic nature.

The indivisibility of the ultimate and the relative:
All phenomena without exception
arise from what has no ground or root;
they are neither existent nor nonexistent.
The ultimate and the relative are indivisible:
the unconditioned itself appears as the conditioned;
in its very appearing there is no substance,
and that very absence of substance is manifold,
like an illusion or a mirage.

Equality of the five great elements as the five Tathāgatas:
From earth, water, fire, wind and space
various marvels come to arise;
they have no ground or root,
but arise thus by way of the nature itself.
The appearance of the individual families
is always equal as great means;
their individual characteristics
are equal in the state of the expanse of wisdom.

Equality of the eight consciousnesses as the five wisdoms:
The all-ground that is not conceptual,
without intrinsic nature, the ground of meaning:
this is called the dharmadhātu,
the wisdom of suchness.

All phenomena without exception
are not grasped as "I" or as self,
and since all of them are indivisible,
all is in the manner of a mirror.
In all things there is fleeting thought,
yet nothing to conceive or examine;
unperceived throughout the three times:
this is the wisdom of equality.
Having abandoned wrong conceptions, the sphere of the faculties,
the objects of the faculties being without substance,
all indivisible, suchness:
this is the discriminating wisdom.
The five equalities are examined thus.
Regarding all phenomena without exception
as without intrinsic nature from the very beginning,
and as great means, spontaneously accomplished:
this is true realization.

The samaya called "The Maṇḍala of the Gathering":
The accumulations of merit and wisdom being complete,
the centre is self-awareness, without edge or centre,
and the four wisdoms radiate in the manner of a wheel.
Or else Vajrasattva himself,
the gods and goddesses of the four families,
and the wrathful gods and goddesses of the retinue.
Or else the centre is the transcendent itself,
and the various worlds are its retinue.

The samaya called "The Feast Offering":
When one is endowed with means and wisdom,
and the mind of awakening and non-attachment
have dissolved into nonduality,
one is endowed with the completion of the two great accumulations as well.
All the five sense pleasures,
and all the various appearances without exception,
are regarded as the maṇḍala of the Tathāgatas,
as gods and goddesses of beautiful form.
All sounds without exception
are the great sound of the Dharma;
they purify the lower realms and show the path to liberation.
They are the great sound that resounds through the three-thousandfold universe,
and the sound that encompasses all.
The merit that satisfies with bliss
is inexpressible, non-abiding wisdom:
this is the speech of the unattached Lotus.
To the nature of reality, which is without substance,
to give supreme satisfaction with fragrance
is said to be a great accumulation of merit;
it is the way of unattached wisdom.
Likewise, if one pleases and makes offering with fragrance,
the levels of the vidyādharas are spurred on.
In the three lower realms, the fragrance of the Dharma
brings attainment of the state of perfect bliss.
All the maṇḍalas of the Sugata without exception
come to be offered the taste of bliss.
Satisfying them becomes a great accumulation of merit;
not grasping it as a thing is the accumulation of merit.
By such a supreme taste
all without exception come to be satisfied;
in the realms of the pretas there is enjoyment.
It is an offering to all the maṇḍalas without exception;
it is the completion of great bliss.
When yogins who are fit for the work
adorn themselves in many ways
and enjoy through the offerings of union,
inconceivable maṇḍalas become spontaneously present.
The maṇḍala of great bliss blazes forth
and dispels the black darkness of evil,
and great powers that no host of armies can withstand
are accomplished as well.

The samaya called "The Activity of the Feast":
In the manner of non-action,
act toward all without hindrance.
All without exception is tamed,
the hostile side is brought under control,
great qualities are increased,
and even the fierce wrathful ones are pacified.
Acting toward all at ease,
one abides in the manner of non-attachment.
It is the maṇḍala gathered into the expanse,
encircled by a retinue of many emanations and reabsorptions.
The state of great equality itself
abides in such a manner.

The samaya called "Satisfying the Phramen of the Retinue":
Oneself as the lord who tames,
always in equipoise in wrathful form,
and the wrathful ones of the retinue likewise,
with all their colours and mudrās,
recite all the supreme and holy mantras without exception.
Then, gathering the retinue of phramen,
and acting in accordance with their individual activities,
place each in its own position.
The methods of bringing under control and of meditation
should be done as is fitting.
Commanding all without exception, "Act!",
give them the remainders and confer empowerment upon them too.
Moreover, to all beings
give the gift of the five sense pleasures.
Then, having completed the activities,
and relying on the abode of Maheśvara,
the five families, the five wisdoms and the rest,
all the maṇḍalas of the retinue without exception,
should all be gathered into oneself.
One who holds the state of practice in this way,
having perfected wisdom and the two accumulations,
abides on the level of the vidyādhara's awareness.
One will not come to the conduct of the lesser vehicle,
will not be born in the unfree states,
and attains the eternal state beyond birth and death.
One purifies the buddha fields as well,
empties the abodes of the lower realms,
and accomplishes the essence of the true and supreme.

All that appears as objects of the faculties:
the objects do not exist;
the all-base consciousness itself
appears as various things.
When appearing things are taken apart,
having taken apart their individual characteristics,
once taken apart, they subside into the state of space.
When that very space is taken apart by way of the five,
it dissolves into the expanse without characteristics,
the source of all.

The instant without ground or root,
not blocking appearance, is skillful means;
the absence of intrinsic nature is wisdom itself.
The afflicted consciousness,
toward the all-base itself appearing as its field of experience,
conceives of that as a self,
and grasps separately at the signs of pleasure and pain.
In the all-base, which has no entity,
there is no place where habitual tendencies accumulate;
since appearing things have no entity,
there is nothing for the afflicted mind to take up.
There is no imprinting and nothing to be imprinted;
thus there is no entity that imprints.
Though nothing is there, the mind falsely imagines,
and so circles forever in the prison of the three realms.
The mental consciousness,
toward both what appears and what does not appear,
is conceptual thought lasting an instant, a moment.
If thought itself is not established,
how could the entities that are its objects be established?
Likewise the very continuum of the mind:
the former moment has passed and is not observed,
the later has not arisen and cannot be examined;
thus its intrinsic nature cannot be observed.
Like wind drifting over the plants and trees,
the continuum is never broken at all;
this is what arises everywhere.
Thus objects have no entity.
Like water, wind, and waves,
those three are neither one nor separate;
likewise the all-base, the afflicted mind, and the mental consciousness
are not one and are not separate.
The expanse of phenomena and the expanse of equality
are all indivisible from wisdom,
and cannot even be spoken of as one.
Not realizing this is to be a sentient being;
when it is realized, buddha is nowhere else.
The suchness of the mind of awakening
is utterly unobservable, hard to examine,
spacious, vast, and profound;
it is realized through profound wisdom.
It has come from nowhere and goes nowhere;
coming and going have no part in it.
Utterly hard to examine, unobservable,
neither its edge nor its center can be found.
Its very self, free from apprehended and apprehender,
appears thus in the manner of the unobservable.
The expanse and the wisdom of the expanse
come down to self-awareness alone.
All sentient beings without exception,
understand it in this way!
You will be free of affliction and suffering
and will attain the unsurpassed fruit.
Toward all sentient beings without exception,
with the cord of compassion, free of attachment,
free them from the path of affliction
and delight them with loving-kindness.
Likewise, into the prison of saṃsāra
cast far the lasso of compassion
and draw them out of the mire of affliction.
Great compassion is not made to arise.
Likewise, among ordinary people,
one is not stained by the obscurations of karma;
seeing all as manifestly awakened,
in joy there is no regret.
Since sentient beings have no "I" and no self,
compassion makes no dualistic distinctions:
set your mind on them as on an only child,
and make them all free of stain.
Since all phenomena are empty,
there is no entity and no concept in them.
Abiding in the state of space,
there is nothing in it to alter or change.
All phenomena are without characteristics;
since all appearances have no entity,
all existence and nonexistence whatever,
being without characteristics, are not observed.
Transcendent and mundane
phenomena, though they appear in many forms,
are not other than oneself;
so there is nothing anywhere to long for or cling to.
All minds of awakening,
being utterly hard to examine and profound,
are not the domain of logicians:
unobserved, not observing, nothing to be observed.
What is the very nature of mind like?
Awakening itself is mind.
Since mind and awakening are indivisible,
all sentient beings without exception
and all phenomena of the three times are this.
All phenomena whatsoever, without exception,
are gathered into mind and awakening itself.
So the great Bodhi has declared.
A person endowed with supreme skill of mind
acts without obstruction toward anything;
in acting itself there is no attachment,
and he abides undiminished for endless time.
All these appearances,
though always seen by valid direct perception,
have no entity in their very appearing;
realize them likewise as the genuine itself.
Whoever says, "A state of emptiness exists,"
and so realizes a nature of nonexistence:
a rabbit's horn and a barren woman's son
may well be designated as mere words,
but all are mere names; there is no emptiness as a thing.
It is said that all without exception are empty,
yet since this is not uttered without awareness,
it is nonconceptual and by nature luminous;
all these appearances
are not phenomena of mere expression;
they are cognition alone.
When all is realized as nondual,
that itself is the ultimate expanse;
it is not to be sought or posited elsewhere.
If it is sought in existence, it becomes superimposition;
if it is sought in nonexistence, it becomes denial;
if it is sought in both existence and nonexistence,
it becomes the undeclared.
He has no conceptual thought,
yet there is nothing he does not know.
Such is the view of yoga.
Though in words there is a before and an after,
in meaning meditate on it as complete all at once.
Once such a meaning is realized,
then even while walking, lying down, sitting, staying, and enjoying things,
one never falls away from the meditation of the Great Vehicle.
Those of poor intellect who have not realized the meaning given above,
having cited equality as their authority,
without realization, lazy and idle,
go to the lower realms without accomplishing the aim.
If the mind has not realized equality,
one does not awaken through an equality of words.
Therefore join meaning and words together!
Then it will be accomplished without doubt.
The yogin who has realized the great meaning
meditates thus for endless time.
His qualities are equal to the limits of space,
his domain is one with the buddhas themselves;
ever endowed with a great accumulation of merit,
through diligence that involves no doing,
he reaches the seat of awakening.

Thus it is in secret words.

This is the seventy-seventh chapter of the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion, teaching the gathering into faculties and objects.

Then the Tathāgata, in order to make the mantra holders' practice meaningful, gathered everything of The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality into the mudrā, examined in this way the whole of the outer and inner, the vessel and its contents, and taught the keeping of the commitment of the mudrā: the commitment is the ground. Condensing and elaborating by means of the mudrā are also this. He then spoke this inspired utterance:

From that very secret alone
this supreme awakening is taught.

The maṇḍala of great equality:
all that has passed and not passed
in the ten directions and four times without exception
is primordially indivisible suchness.
It is not something to be made equal or unequal;
it is gathered into the state of suchness.
All expressions without exception,
transcendent and mundane,
are not realized apart from the state of the nature of reality;
there is not an atom of wrong realization or affliction.
Though the various mudrās blaze with light,
they have not moved from suchness,
because the self is not other than the expanse.
Meditating in the threefold way
of abiding equally in equality,
generating the mudrās and taming the perverse,
in a manner with neither act nor agent
one completes all activities without exception.
Realizing thus, the supreme of the family,
oneself the self of all the mudrās,
being primordially luminous by nature,
is also the cause from which awakening is born,
and is the result, as the genuine itself.
Cause and result indivisible, the vajra ground,
the thirteenth, where the aim is accomplished: abiding there,
it is the ultimate, abiding nowhere at all.
The natural great mudrā:
all the outer and inner, the vessel and its contents,
all abide in the state of wisdom.
As the Sugatas of the five families in union, father and mother,
uncontrived, they abide thus from the beginning,
and play in skillful means and wisdom themselves:
the great assembly, beyond bliss and unattached,
perfectly complete in the genuine itself.
The surpassing great mudrā:
the inner sign has emerged outward.
In brief, the great mudrā
and also the various other mudrās,
the inconceivable gathering and radiating of the hands,
the great postures of the limbs, all their moving and swaying,
though they do not move from their own expanse,
abide thus by their very nature.
Conquering, unstoppable by anything,
the great self-arisen one, god of gods,
pierces through everything;
he is also to be designated "the Hero."
Unstained by karma and by action,
realizing the meaning of the nondual expanse,
attached to non-attachment,
in the utterly pure
maṇḍala of the expanse of suchness,
endowed with body, speech, and mind, never exhausted,
he becomes an inexhaustible treasure of qualities.
The ground is endowed with inexhaustibility;
he brings to completion the activities of body and speech.
In a nonconceptual manner
he plays without attachment in all things.
His ways of benefiting those to be tamed are inconceivable:
conquering, unattached, the source of all,
bringing all under power, and accomplishing aims;
all gathered without exception, completely pure,
round on every side and radiating rays of light.
The four families, all without exception,
are all gathered into the expanse of equal perfection.
The families are one without being one,
in the very state of the spontaneously present maṇḍala.
The defining mark of the commitment of the mudrā
is taught as the sign of the mind.
For example, when a king sets his seal,
no sentient being whatsoever transgresses it;
likewise equality is the seal:
it pervades all, and all appears.
When sealed with body, speech, and mind,
the māras and the many non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), all the adverse side,
cannot gain power over one.
One who is certain in the family of Vajrasattva
always abides in such a way;
one without such understanding
is never said to belong to the vajra family.

This is the seventy-eighth chapter of The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, on the commitment of the mudrā.

Then the Blessed One, Great Delight, spoke this firmly held king of secret mantras as an inspired utterance to the tathāgatas themselves:

Kye kye! This nature of the ten directions and four times
is the very essence of the tathāgatas.
Clinging to the elaborations of apprehended and apprehender,
beings are each caught by the noose of concepts.
The grounds too, set out in their distinctions,
are the path that leads to the secret essence.
The ocean of wondrous wisdom
has never spoken anything that would not become meaningful.
Whoever speaks this secret great mudrā,
this essence, having realized it through all the tathāgatas,
that very one is I, and his empowerment too is complete.
All the tathāgatas
have no secret other than this.
The secret essence, reality,
which pacifies superimposition and denial,
was drawn out of the expanse that ascertains reality.
This is certainly prophesied:
the son is born from the heart,
and abides on the ground of the supreme awareness holder.

When this had been said, all the tathāgatas, indivisibly delighted, remained as the very ornament arrayed as the dense spontaneous presence of body, speech, and mind, the expanse of the equality of the four times.

This is the seventy-ninth chapter of The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, on the entrustment.

From the Noble King, the Net of Illusion in One Hundred Thousand, the general scripture of all the characteristics and all the tantras, the secret of all the tathāgatas: from the chapters of The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, everything has been elaborated without exception, and this is the supreme fruit of those wheels that were turned.

Since this great secret exists,
even across a pit dug full of burning embers
and packed with razor edges,
climb over it, my children, and go!
In the innermost of the inner
an iron casket is sealed shut.
Though, like a mirror, it is looked at by all,
no reflection appears.
With the magic key of wisdom
open the vajra-bolted casket,
draw out the treasure of wish-fulfilling jewels,
and you, the supremely fortunate, please enjoy it!
It does not appear in letters for all;
abiding in the heart of the master,
it is the domain of the supremely fortunate
and is not shared with everyone.
For example, the milk of a lioness
can be held in a golden cup,
but if it is poured into other vessels,
the vessel cracks and the milk leaks away.
In the very nature of gold,
whatever distinctions of shape may appear,
there is not an atom that has changed from gold.
Likewise the defining character of phenomena:
whatever various signs appear,
there is not an atom that has changed from itself.

This completes the eightieth chapter of The Secret Essence, Ascertaining Reality, teaching that the nature of reality never changes.

Translator's notes

The title is given only in Tibetan here; it corresponds to the Guhyagarbha Tattvaviniścaya. ཆོས་ཀྱིས་དབྱིངས is read as ཆོས་ཀྱི་དབྱིངས, "expanse of reality". "The secret assembly" (གསང་འདུས) may allude to the Guhyasamāja, but the phrase is taken in its plain sense. The paired epithets for Samantabhadra and Samantabhadrī contrast the agent (བྱེད་པ་པོ) with the feminine "she who is the action" (བྱ་བ་མོ). གཡས་གཡོགས is read as གཡའ་གཡོགས, "covered with rust", which the image of the mirror requires; འཇིག་རྟོག in the closing verses is read as འཇིག་རྟེན, "world". The chapter colophon of chapter five names the work the King of Tantras, the Net of Illusion (Māyājāla), the cycle to which the Secret Essence belongs. In the verses on the parts of a letter, ཚིག is read as ཚེག, the dot that separates syllables, since it stands between the head of the letter and the dividing stroke (ཤད). "All of them, through the female bodhisattvas, are gathered into the secret vajra faculty" follows the text as written (byang chub sems ma yi); some witnesses read byang chub sems kyi, which would give "all are gathered into the secret vajra faculty of bodhicitta." "Universal Light and Lotus Eye" names the eleventh and twelfth grounds of the Nyingma scheme; the usual reading is "Lotus-Possessor" (padma can), and here the text has padma spyan, rendered as written; the Wheel of the Garland of Letters is the thirteenth ground. In "the inner and the outer," the text's nad ("disease") is taken as a slip for nang ("inner"), which the context plainly requires. The list of thrones names both khyung and nam mkha' lding, ordinarily two words for the garuḍa; five thrones are needed for the five families, so the second is given as "the sky-soaring bird" (in other versions this place is held by the peacock and the civacivaka bird). "The doer and the lord of deeds" renders bya ba and bya bdag literally; commentaries identify the pair stationed in front and behind as Samantabhadra and Samantabhadrī. "Golden yellow" renders ser le brgan, whose last element is uncertain. "The male Vajra Agent and the female Vajra Action" renders byed pa po rdo rje and bya ba mo rdo rje, the masculine and feminine principles of the maṇḍala, identified in the commentaries with Samantabhadra and Samantabhadrī. "Like the clearing of a sneeze" renders sbrid sangs bzhin as it stands; the image is obscure and may reflect a corrupt reading. "Nothing to destroy" renders gsad, literally "to kill"; it may stand for bsal, "to remove". In the mudrā verses, "syllables" (literally "grains") and "letters" designate the fingers, and the sun and moon the right and left hands. "The ringing staff" renders gseg par, which is uncertain; the six emblems of the six sages are traditionally the lute, armor, staff and bowl, book, jewel casket, and fire and water. In "without departing from his own body," the text's ma dgod ("without laughing") is taken as a slip for a verb of departing, which the simile requires. Here འདུལ་བ is "taming" (subjugation of hostile forces), not the monastic Vinaya. In the karma section ལས་ཀྱི་སེམས་དང ("with the karma mind") is read as ལས་ཀྱི་སེམས་དཔར ("as the Karma Being"), following the parallel lines of the other families, and མ་གཡིས as མ་གཡོས ("unmoved"). The "-dhara" names (Cakradhara, Padmadhara, Ratnadhara, Khaḍgadhara: wheel-, lotus-, jewel- and sword-holder) follow the pattern of Vajradhara in the preceding piece. The ringing-rite lines are terse, and the sense of not passing beyond and just passing beyond oṃ and haṃ, apparently levels or stages of the gesture, is uncertain. ཡིན་ལ་དམིགས is read as ཡིད་ལ་དམིགས ("what the mind is set on"). ཡུམ་དང་འཇོ་མ་སེམས་མ is uncertain; the consorts, bestowing goddesses and attendant (offering) goddesses are a tentative rendering. The white tent, square, red crescent and black blazing correspond to the four activities of pacifying, enriching, magnetizing and wrathful action. In the rite of taming, འདུལ་བ is "taming", not the monastic Vinaya. ཐུས་ཉིད is read as ཐུགས་ཉིད ("very mind"). In the verses on the vajra and bell, many lines are highly condensed ritual instructions and the syntax of several is uncertain. "Within sixteen lifetimes at most" interprets the bare "within sixteen" (དེ་ཚེ་བཅུ་དྲུག་ཚུན་ཆད) in line with the usual Mahāyoga promise of awakening within sixteen lives. ལྷག་པའི in the closing sentence is rendered as "special teaching from"; it may instead qualify the image ("the superior image"). The four rites here are the standard four activities: black for wrathful destruction, red (Hayagrīva) for magnetizing, gold for enriching, white for pacifying. The "wheels" of hearing, clarity, the hands, speech and great movement appear to be the sense and action faculties (ear, eye, hands, tongue, and feet or movement) visualized as palaces; "wheel of clarity" (གསལ་བའི་འཁོར་ལོ) for the eye is an interpretation. The line about not abiding in equality (མཉམ་པར་མཉམ་ལ་མི་གནས་པའི) is terse and its rendering tentative. ཨ་མ ("from the mother") might instead be the syllables a and ma; it is read as the ordinary word "mother," following the bhaga imagery. The line ཞི་བ་ཕྱོགས་གཅིག་ཤེས་བྱ་སྤྲོ is obscure; the rendering is tentative. བདེ་གཤེགས་འབྱུང་དང་སྐོལ་མི་འགྱུར is read as "will not be parted from the arising of the sugatas," taking སྐོལ as "apart." The four realizations at the start (the single cause, the mode of the syllables, blessing, and direct perception) are the standard Guhyagarbha set of four realizations. "Those who have become fields" renders zhing 'gyur, the beings who are fit objects for the rite of liberation. "Uṣṇīṣavijaya" renders gtsug tor rnam par rgyal ba; it may instead be read as "the victorious crown" visualized on the purified being. "The elements are the consorts of the wisdom family" reads ཤེས་རབ for the written ཞེས་རབ, an evident copyist's slip. The four kinds of secret meaning listed early in the chapter are rendered as the tradition explains them (secret for discipline, in intent, concealed, by nature); the Tibetan is terse here. The line "We do not awaken by searching elsewhere" keeps the first-person plural of the Tibetan. The verses at the end of this piece are elliptical and the rendering follows the most natural syntax. The first line of "How wonderful! All beings..." reads literally "all beings are not in the sugatas"; it is rendered as "not apart from the sugatas," which fits the doctrine of the passage, but the Tibetan is terse. "The uninterrupted hell of Extreme Heat" renders mtshams med pa'i rab tu tsha ba; it may refer to Avīci and Pratāpana together. "The casket" renders za ma tog (Skt. karaṇḍa). In the final sentence the one who knows is unstated in the Tibetan and is rendered with a passive. Several goddess names are given in Sanskrit where the Tibetan is a recognized rendering (Brahmāṇī, Raudrī, Indrāṇī, Vaiṣṇavī, Kaumārī, Amṛtā, Śāntī, Daṇḍī, Rākṣasī, Bhakṣasī, Ratī, Rudhiramadī, Ekacāriṇī, Manohārikā, Siddhikarī, Vāyudevī, Agnāyī, Vārāhī, Cāmuṇḍī, Varuṇānī, Mahākālī, Kumbhakarṇī). Those I could not identify with confidence are translated literally: the Red One (dmar mo), the Slayer (gsod byed mo), the Noseless One (sna chad mo), the Goat-Headed One (ra mgo), and the Belly-Holder (gsus 'dzin, perhaps Lambodarā). In the second list mi srun mo is taken as a variant of mi'i srin mo (Manurākṣasī), gru mo as grub mo (Siddhikarī), and the great Red-Yellow, Blue-Black, and Yellow-Black Ones as the last three goddesses of the first list. The interjection thong thong is rendered "Hey, hey." The eight goddess classes are given by their usual Sanskrit names (Gaurī, Caurī, Pramohā, Vetālī, Pukkasī, Caṇḍālī, Ghasmarī), taking thal byed ma as Vetālī and sme sha can as Ghasmarī; these identifications follow the usual peaceful-wrathful lists but are not certain. The four classes at the gates (rdo rje bsrings 'gro ma, rdo rje gdong mo, rdo rje 'jig rten ma, rdo rje ro langs ma) are translated literally. The phrase rendered "each in an instant took up her own place" (rang rang gi gnas ga la bar) is obscure. Ucchuṣma Krodha is given as a name. The line on the limbs and heart with five, nine and eleven prongs is terse; it appears to describe visualizing the body's limbs and heart as multi-pronged vajras, but the construction is uncertain. "Stira" in the eastern and western implements is left as a loanword; it is an unidentified Indic term for a staff or weapon. In the west, "the great oceans cut through at the waist" may refer to the nāgas of the ocean rather than the waters themselves. "The Guardian of Words" translates an epithet for the northwestern protector that I cannot identify with confidence (the northwest is normally Vāyu's direction). The "great boar" held for the nadir may allude to Varāha, but this is not certain. "Red" in the drinking passages renders the Tibetan "red," a standard tantric term for blood (rakta). "Stira" is an unidentified Indic loanword for a staff or weapon, kept as in the previous piece. "The great transcendent sūtra" may be the title of a specific scripture on the mātṛkās, which I cannot identify with confidence. The vajra secret words of chapter thirty-one are deliberately cryptic; the rendering follows the Tibetan closely, and several lines (notably the one on rebirth-linkings and the line on the husk) are uncertain in their construction. "Handmaidens and serving women" renders two terms for classes of female attendants at the gates whose exact identity is uncertain. In "not giving to others," the text's me gtong is read as mi gtong, an evident copyist's slip; likewise zig gnon is read as zil gnon, "overpower," and cing yang as ci yang, "anything." The line on "the great delusion that does not spread" is obscure in its construction. The "two clusters" of great deities is rendered literally; it likely refers to two groupings of the wrathful maṇḍala. This chapter is a dense tantric hymn associating the five poisons with the five wrathful bodies, and several lines are compressed to the point of uncertainty. In chapter thirty-three the stanza for the jewel family appears twice in identical form where one might expect a further family (such as the lotus); it is translated as it stands. "In one hundred thousand" in the thirty-second chapter colophon reflects the Tibetan's reference to a version of the text in a hundred thousand sections or verses. Several ritual phrases in chapter thirty-four are uncertain: "the mother for knowing and entering," "the colors of ignorance," "the one who has passed beyond" (which may denote either the transcendent deity or a deceased person), and "the supreme and the lesser transformed fields" (possibly a term for corpses or charnel substances) are rendered literally. "Never" reads the text's nams kyang as nam yang. The king first called "King of the Vast Earth" and then "the great Māra, king of the Earth-Holders" is rendered literally; I cannot identify a Sanskrit name with confidence. The line describing some beings "with their whole bodies" appears to lack a word after "utterly," and "pinned" renders an obscure term; both are uncertain. "Hey, hey" renders an exclamation (thong thong) of uncertain sense. The phrase "the sacred teaching" is written twice in the Tibetan. The "animal-headed goddesses" render ཕྲ་མེན, the therianthropic phramen goddesses of the Nyingma maṇḍalas. Several lines of the chapter thirty seven verse are terse and their sense is uncertain, notably "lay the lines with the soles of the feet", "like a cast image" (ལུགས་ཀ), and "lending" (གཞོན, read as a lending or pledging transaction). In "one who wishes to become habituated will attain this", ཐོད་པ ("skull") is read as ཐོབ་པ ("attain"), which the following list of powers requires. "Great corpses" (ཞིང་ཆེན) are human corpses used as seats. In the final verse, the last stanza has ཚོགས ("host") where the others have ཚོན ("colors"); it is translated as written. "Locanā" renders ལོ་ཙ་ན, taken as the name of the buddha consort Locanā; it could also be the Sanskrit word for "eye." The line rendered "those who destroy the body of merit come under control" (བསོད་ནམས་ལུས་འཇིག་དབང་དུ་འགྱུར) is obscure, and the syllable དབུང in "deep dark blue" is left untranslated as unclear. "The Lord of Desire" (འདོད་དབང) is not confidently identified; it may mean Kāmadeva or the lord of the desire realm. "Poison sent by sorcery" renders སྦོད་གཏོང་དུག, taken as poison deliberately administered or sent by malign rite. The verse beginning "oṃ, by nature great delusion" has only three lines in the Tibetan. Amṛtakuṇḍalin renders བདུད་རྩི་འཁྱིལ་པ, the wrathful deity Nectar Coil. On the eastern and southern spokes the Tibetan names only "Vajra" and "Jewel" (Ratna), presumably standing for the vajra and jewel family buddhas. In the fourth sequence, "Filling those and the body" renders དེ་ཉིད་དག་ལ་ལུས་ཁེངས་པར, which differs from the parallel lines (ངག་ལམ "path of speech"). "Drive him with iron" (ལྕགས་ཀྱིས) is likely an iron hook. The passage ends in mid-sentence; the next piece continues it. canaka is a Sanskrit word in Tibetan letters whose meaning here is unclear; it is left untranslated. Several apparent copyists' slips are translated by their evident readings: "means of entry" for འཛུམ ("smile") where the parallel stanza has འཛུལ; "non-apprehension" for མི་དགྱེས, parallel to མི་དམིགས; "mudrā" for ཕྱག་བྱ; "number" for གྲགས, parallel to གྲངས; and "state" for དང, parallel to ངང. "The image into which the deity is to be brought down" (དབབ་པར་བྱ་བའི་གཟུགས་བརྙན) refers to a rite of causing a deity to descend into an effigy or a tree sapling. The opening line "Those are the deities above" (དེ་ཉིད་སྟེང་གི་ལྷ་རྣམས) is obscure; parallel stanzas name a seed syllable here. Several evident copyists' slips are translated by the reading the parallels require: དོན་ཡོན for དོན་ཡོད (Amoghasiddhi), "vajra deities" for རྡོ་རྗེ་ལ་ལ, "four gatekeeper goddesses" for སྒོམ་བཞི and སྒོ་མ་བཞི, "secret" for གསག, "names" for མི, "abode" for སྣོད་ནས, and "forty second" for བཅི་བཅུ་གཉིས. "Cutting short life" renders ཚེ་བསྟུང, and "snatching away" renders དཔྲོག; both are uncertain. canaka is left as written. The Tibetan ཙ་ན་ཀ is taken as Sanskrit caṇaka, "chickpea," a common size measure, hence "buddhas the size of chickpeas." Several evident scribal slips are read for sense: སྙིག་པོ as སྙིང་པོ "essence," དང་ཚུལ as ངང་ཚུལ "state," and སངས་རྒྱ as སངས་རྒྱས. In the Karma section the syllable མ parallels the seed syllable of the lotus section and is treated as a seed syllable; it could alternatively be read as "mother." The line beginning "mark the mouth and heart of the one toward whom it is done" is overlong and appears conflated in the source; it is translated as it stands. The first part of this piece repeats the lines that close the previous piece, as the source text does. བརྩོམ in "whoever strives in this" is read as བརྩོན "strive," as in the parallel line later. The opening prose listing classes of nāgas and "the second direction" is obscure, and "the Fifth" appears to be the name of this obstacle-removing rite; both are translated literally. ཞིང་སྟེངས "upon the field" may refer to a cremation ground or a corpse seat; the sense is uncertain. The verb in "if one wishes them to leap up" (མཆོངས) contrasts with "if one wishes them to live" (འཚོ) in the parallel line and may be a scribal variant of it. བརྒྱ་ལ་འགྱུར "becomes a hundred" is read as བརྒྱལ་འགྱུར "they will faint," as in the parallel lines. ཐོད་པར་འཐུང་ཟ་མེད་པའི་ནང་ is obscure; it is rendered "drink from skull cups and eat without limit." དྲི་ཟ་མེད་པའི་བདག་པོ is read as དྲི་ཟ་མེ་ཡི་བདག་པོ, "the lord of the gandharvas and the lord of fire," matching the list of direction guardians that follows (Īśāna, the wind, yakṣas, rākṣasas, the lord of the waters, Yama). The figures for the retinues are given as the Tibetan states them. The opening lines in long meter are terse and partly obscure; "beyond the scattering and gathering of seeds, so hard to approach" renders འབྲུ་གཏོར་བསྡུ་བ་ལས་ནི་འདས་བཞིན་བསྙགས་དཀའ་བའི literally. In the list of goddesses, ར་རོ་ཁྲག་འཐུང་མྱོས "intoxicated by drinking blood" is taken as Rudhiramadī; ཀླུང་མོ "River Goddess" may be a slip for རླུང་མོ, the wind goddess (Vāyudevī); མི་མོ "Human Woman" may be a slip for མེ་མོ, the fire goddess (Agnāyī); and གསུས་འཛིན "belly-holder" is taken as Lambodarā, the pot-bellied goddess. These are translated as written, with the identifications offered tentatively. ལང་སྤྱོད་པ is read as བ་ལང་སྤྱོད་པ, the continent Godānīya, in the list of the four continents. སྦྲེབས in "burning and torment" is obscure; it is rendered by sense. The line "set down in five and six times" (ལྔ་དྲུག་དུས་བཏབ) is cryptic and translated literally. In the wrathful context, "liberate" (བསྒྲལ) has its ritual sense of slaying while freeing the consciousness. The line rendered "what they do amasses no karma but becomes the accumulations" reads literally "in what is done, not amassing, it becomes accumulation"; the sense is that action by those certain in the unsurpassed vehicle does not accrue karmic bondage but becomes the accumulations of merit and wisdom. The counts of subdivisions ("two and three tens" for each of the five roots, i.e. thirty-two, and "two tens" for each of the ten branches) total 160 plus 200, the three hundred and sixty samayas named in the chapter colophon. Several names in the final list could not be identified with a Sanskrit form and are translated or given phonetically: Dripping Blood, Black Killer, Black Liberation (Tharpa Nakpo, the rudra of Nyingma tradition), Mujé Tsenpa (a king of the mu spirits), Fierce Youth, Lichin, Hand Roamer, Vajra Beauty, and Yamshü Marpo. "Sthira" renders the Tibetan sti ra, whose sense here is uncertain; it may be a transcribed Indic word for the charnel feast or for corpses. "Transference" renders spo, read as the ritual transfer of the consciousness of the one liberated; it might instead be a seed syllable. "Dispatch offering" renders sbod gtong, whose exact sense is uncertain. The chapter numbering in the colophons runs from fifty-two in the previous piece to fifty-four here, as the text stands. The king of the wrathful ones "Radiating" and the "King Who Increases" are translated from the Tibetan, as no Sanskrit form could be identified with confidence. "The five paths" are taken as the five destinies of beings. e, vaṃ, ma and ya are the four syllables of evaṃ māyā, each governing one of the four rites of wrath, subjugation, increase and pacification. The goddess names "Treasure Holder", "Swollen One", "Lion-Faced One" and "Laughing-Faced One" are translated from the Tibetan, as their Sanskrit forms are not certain. "Kali" is left as written; it may stand for the kīla rite or a related ritual act, and its sense here is uncertain. "Three dawning, five blazing, ten in abundance" renders a cryptic line, probably counting the three bodies, five wisdoms and ten grounds or perfections; it is left literal. The verb rendered "enclosed" (ubs) is uncertain in this context. The nṛ ("man") is the ritual term for the person or effigy on whose behalf the life rite is performed. In the line rendered "conceal it once more within the expanse," the text reads spa ba, taken as sba ba, "to hide." Here the deity's name tshe dbang mo is read as "the goddess of life empowerment," which fits the following lines about granting the life empowerment, and not as Indrāṇī. The name of the Tathāgata rdo rje sdud pa po is translated by meaning ("Vajra Gatherer") because no certain Sanskrit form can be identified. The line rendered "the excellent lines of nṛ, ash, and manta" is obscure; it seems to describe the materials used to draw the maṇḍala lines. The pra rite is a divination: a deity or spirit is made to "descend" into a support, such as a child medium or a mirror, and speak. "Five paths" probably means the five classes of beings. Here the Tibetan word dbang mo, which was given as Indrāṇī earlier, heads a list of worldly powers, so it is read as a class, "sovereign goddesses." "Endowed with a hundred sacrifices" (mchod sbyin brgya) probably alludes to Indra's epithet Śatakratu, "he of a hundred sacrifices." This fits the Indra mantra that follows, although the passage is about Dhṛtarāṣṭra. Vaiśravaṇa's emblem dbyig to is uncertain; it is rendered "jeweled staff." Here the text assigns the deep-red color, the nāgas of the west, and a mantra naming Virūḍhaka to Virūpākṣa, and the translation keeps this as written. The opening sentence breaks off in the Tibetan with no verb, and the translation keeps it as it stands. In "free from conceptualization itself," rtogs ("realization") is read as rtog ("conceptual thought"). The palm-tree simile and the line "the objects and the times are the great ones" are obscure, and their renderings are tentative. "Those of awakening" who are shown "the way of the self-arisen" probably means the pratyekabuddhas. The "five great inner ones" are probably the five afflictions, which are presented here as the five poisons transformed. The phrase phyag rgya chen po ("great seal") is a technical term here, so it is rendered as "great seal" (mahāmudrā) of mind and of speech, and the dharmakāya is likewise called "the great seal." The verses on killing beings who have committed the deeds of immediate retribution (ānantarya) express the tantric doctrine of compassionate "liberation" by one held by skillful means; they are rendered as the text states them. In the jealousy section, "in all its forms" renders ཀུན་གྱི, which may be a slip for རང་གི ("specific"), matching the pattern of the other sections. "Those who conform to permanence" (རྟག་མཐུན) is a literal rendering of an uncertain term, probably a class of non-Buddhist ascetics. "The thought of means" reads རྟོགས་པ as རྟོག་པ, as the context of jealous thought suggests. "The very limit of nirvāṇa" renders མཐའ in the sense of culmination rather than "extreme." རྒྱུད here is taken as "continuum" (the mind-stream), not "tantra," as the line requires. In the list of purified substances, "the bones" renders རུས་པ as written. In "conceiving of things and non-things," རྟོགས་པ is read as རྟོག་པ, as the context of signs requires; likewise ཕུལ in "unlike the ordinary" is read as ཕལ. ཚངས་པར་སྤྱད་པ is the holy life (brahmacarya), not the god Brahmā. The lines describing the dwelling place ("great skillful means, the all-accomplishing, luminous love; the all-delighting, fierce lady of the expanse") may name the principal deity and consort by epithets; they are rendered descriptively since no names can be identified with confidence. "Supremely adorned" reads སྤགས as སྤྲས. "Ah" renders the exclamation ཨ་རེ. ཞིང, literally "field," is the tantric code word for a corpse in this rite and is translated "corpse." ནྲྀ (nṛ, Sanskrit for "human") is a code term left as written; it appears to denote human substance or the human corpse. Several details are uncertain: the description "long, yellow, and glossy" (སྣར་དང་སེར་དང་མདོག་སྣུམ), the duration units for "nine, twelve, thirteen, or up to sixteen" (the text gives none; probably days), "the corpse of the five roads" (ལམ་ལྔའི་ཞིང), "mottled" for བན་ལང་ཅན, and the terms de byi pu tra and a nṛ bri du, which are kept as written. "Great conch without seams" may be a code for a skull without sutures. "Gods, mātṛkās, and rākṣasas" takes ལྷ་མ་སྲིན as the triad lha, mamo, and srinpo. "The Unshakable" renders མི་འཁྲུགས་པ, possibly Akṣobhya. The chapter-title term བྲོ་གར་རྩེ་འཇོ is rendered literally as dance and song, play. The text reads "the faculty of the nose" (སྣ) for the second maṇḍala, but the verses that follow concern hearing and sound; this is translated as the evident reading རྣ, "ear." "The direction of qualities" is the south, the direction of the jewel family; the west is described with a play on ནུབ, "set" or "decline." བདེ་མཆོག here is "supreme bliss," not the deity Cakrasaṃvara. "The Unattached" (མ་ཆགས) may be an epithet of a female deity. "They will not miss the appearance of the buddhas" renders སངས་རྒྱས་འབྱུང་དང་སྐར་མི་འགྱུར, where སྐར is read as standing for སྐལ or a word meaning "parted from"; the sense of not being cut off from the advent of buddhas seems clear. "The strength of great might" reads ཚན་པོ་ཆེ as བཙན་པོ་ཆེ. "A mountain utterly firm" reads ཤིན་ཏུ་བསྟན་པ as ཤིན་ཏུ་བརྟན་པ, which the following line about the winds of non-Buddhists supports. The eṇaya is the black antelope, alluding to the Buddha's manifestation among animals as a deer. "The All-Victorious One" renders ཀུན་དུ་རྒྱལ, apparently an epithet of the speaking buddha; it is translated rather than identified. In the seventy-third chapter title, བྱ་བརྒྱུད is read as "the lineage that is to be kept"; the phrase is compressed and the reading is tentative. "The eye of nṛ" keeps the seed syllable as written; it likely evokes a human eye, that is, visualizing so vividly that it seems visible to ordinary sight. The line "never has it been said that they fail to become the aim" renders a terse phrase (དོན་དུ་མི་འགྱུར་ཡོང་མ་གསུངས) whose exact sense is uncertain. Here བདེ་མཆོག is rendered "supreme bliss" rather than as the deity name Cakrasaṃvara, since in this tantra it describes the realized maṇḍala, not that deity. The list of hearth shapes (triangle, skull cup, crescent, lotus, "the one free of attachment") is given as written; how the shapes match the four rites is not stated in this passage. The verses on jealousy play on the word "narrow" contained in the Tibetan for jealousy. "The eight and eight, sixteen" are the eight hot and eight cold hells, as the following lines make clear. "Those without memory or speech" refers to animals, whose abodes this samaya empties. As in the previous chapter, བདེ་མཆོག in "Pride, Supreme Bliss" is taken as "supreme bliss," not the deity Cakrasaṃvara. "The way of outcasts" renders གདོལ་པ་ཅན་གྱི་ཆོས, taken as conduct of a debased kind; its exact point here is not certain. "One's age is that of sixteen, and one remains, prolonging it for hundreds upon hundreds of years" interprets two terse lines (ཚེ་ནི་བཅུ་དང་དྲུག་པ་ཡིན། བརྒྱ་བརྒྱ་དག་ཏུ་བསྣུར་ཞིང་གནས), taken as the vidyādhara keeping a youthful body and long life; the reading is tentative. In the line "This is the supreme speech of wisdom," the Tibetan has an extra syllable (ཡེ་ཤེས་གས་གསུང་མཆོག), evidently a copyist's dittography; it is read as ཡེ་ཤེས་གསུང་མཆོག. The heading ཡུམ་འགྱུར་དབྱིངས་ཕྱུག་མ lacks the usual "called" and its first element (ཡུམ་འགྱུར, "become the Mother/consort") is uncertain in construction. ཆེ་མཆོག in the ninth heading may allude to the deity Mahottara (Chemchok), but is rendered here by its plain sense, "Great and Supreme." In "appear separately as marks of understanding," the Tibetan has རྟོགས་པ ("realization, understanding"); it may stand for རྟོག་པ ("conceptualization"), which would give "marks of conception." The heading ཐབས་གཉིས་ཤེས་རབ is rendered as written ("The Two, Skillful Means and Wisdom"); the pairing with the next heading suggests it may have meant "Means Itself as Wisdom" (ཐབས་ཉིད). In this tantra "liberation" (bsgral ba) is the technical term of wrathful rites for killing while freeing the consciousness; the text here interprets it as the liberation of conceptual signs into the expanse. "The great self" renders བདག་ཉིད་ཆེན་པོ, the "great identity" of all things. In "yet there is no grasping thing at all," ཡོང་མེད is read as ཡོངས་མེད ("not at all"). In "Unattached Lotus," བསྒོ is taken in its sense "to stain, defile," as the lotus image requires, rather than "to command." "The precious wealth of awakening" reads པོ་དེའི་དཀོར as བོ་དེའི་དཀོར, with bo de for Sanskrit bodhi; this reading is uncertain. In the last line of "The Burning Fire of Wisdom," ཚོགས ("gathered") is read as ཚིག ("burned"), which the fire image plainly calls for. The line rendered "Like a child whose strength has not unfolded" (ཇི་ལྟར་བུས་པ་ཤེད་མ་བྱེ) is obscure; བུས་པ is taken as བྱིས་པ ("child"), and the rendering is tentative. "The three supreme ones" (མཆོག་གསུམ) is translated literally; it most likely refers to the highest classes of vehicle or tantra in the Nyingma ninefold scheme. "Blazing like fire" reads the unclear med dbal as me dbal, "fire-blaze." The "arika" whose flesh is assigned to the Lotus family could not be identified with confidence and is left as spelled. "Great flesh" is the standard tantric term for human flesh. Amogha is short for Amoghasiddhi, head of the karma family. "Thousandfold world-systems" renders stong khams; it might also be read as "the element of emptiness." "The four name aggregates" are feeling, perception, formations and consciousness, the four non-physical aggregates. The "sense sources" (āyatana) here are the four formless spheres. "Without realization" reads ma rtogs pa zhes as a quoted phrase used adverbially. "Abiding in nothing" reads ji la as ci la. The "seven riches" (nor bdun) in the last lines are probably the seven noble riches. The contrast in that verse is obscure. "Overwhelming buddha-fields" renders mi bzad. Mi zad, "inexhaustible," may be the intended reading. In the list of five elements, water is not named separately in this text as it stands. The "meeting of the three" is the meeting of object, sense faculty and consciousness. The "fifty-one" formations are the mental factors. Exactly what the "eight roots" are is not clear from the passage. Ser ba here plainly means the colour yellow, in a list of colours, and not hail. The "five directions" passage puns on the Tibetan direction names: shar, east, is "rising"; nub, west, is "setting"; and byang, north, is "purified." The south is not named in this text. "Self and deity are then two" renders bdag dang lha nyid gnyis pa'o literally; its intended force is unclear. "With nothing to be cured" renders sman pa med tentatively. "The four" in the opening line is presumably the four aggregates of the preceding verses. The phramen, here and in the samaya's title, are the animal-headed goddesses of the wrathful retinue. No Sanskrit name for them could be identified with confidence, so the Tibetan term is kept. "By way of the five" in the final verse renders lngas; what the five are is left unstated in the text. "Fleeting thought" renders yud rtog. The three likened to water, wind, and waves are given in the Tibetan simply as the all-base, the afflicted, and mind; following the preceding verses, these are taken as the all-base consciousness, the afflicted mind, and the mental consciousness. "Great compassion is not made to arise" renders སྐྱེ་མ་བྱེད literally; it may mean that great compassion is not contrived, or the negative may be a scribal slip for "generate great compassion." རྟགས in "when all is realized as nondual" is read as རྟོགས, and the obscure འོན་ཏང is taken as "then." "The great Bodhi" (བོ་དྷི་ཆེན་པོ) is a transliterated Sanskrit word; whether it names a speaker or awakening personified is unclear. In the closing prose, དོན་ཡོད is here the ordinary word "meaningful," not the name Amogha. "Beyond bliss and unattached" renders the obscure བདེ་མིན་མ་ཆགས; it may be a slip for བདེ་ཆེན, "great bliss." "Where the aim is accomplished" takes དོན་གྲུབ in its ordinary sense; it may also allude to Amoghasiddhi. The five epithets "conquering, unattached, the source of all, bringing all under power, and accomplishing aims" appear to correspond to the activities of the five families. "The Noble King, the Net of Illusion in One Hundred Thousand" refers to the larger Māyājāla cycle from which this tantra is said to be drawn.
